Batalin-Vilkovisky quantization with an angular twist


Abstract
We construct cubic scalar field theory on λ \lambda -Minkowski space by combining the Batalin-Vilkovisky formalism with harmonic analysis, and produce two inequivalent noncommutative quantum field theories. The braided theory is based on a braided L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra whereby covariance dictates a spectral decomposition into cylindrical Bessel functions that diagonalise the angular Drinfel’d twist; in this theory we find the usual logarithmic ultraviolet divergences and confirm the absence of UV/IR mixing. The standard noncommutative theory is based on a classical L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra; in this theory we relate the spectral decompositions into plane wave and cylindrical harmonic eigenmodes of the Klein-Gordan operator, we verify the planar equivalence theorem, and we demonstrate a periodic form of UV/IR mixing in which non-planar correlators are generically ultraviolet finite but become non-analytic on an infinite lattice of exceptional momenta.

\lambda
-Minkowski space, scalar field theory, BV quantization

1
Introduction

Noncommutative field theories have been of interest for decades due to their role as effective theories spanning condensed matter physics to string theory as well as other models of quantum gravity (see e.g.
[
19
,
39
,
26
,
44
,
40
]
for reviews). As quantum field theories they have introduced new concepts and revealed novel physical phenomena. One of their most intriguing and at the same time pathological features is ultraviolet/infrared (UV/IR) mixing: high-energy divergences reappear as low-energy divergences in higher-loop diagrams. UV/IR mixing was first observed in
[
34
]
in the context of scalar field theories with Moyal noncommutative deformation, and was subsequently discussed in more intricate models of noncommutative spacetimes. Open problems include conceptual issues surrounding the renormalizablity of scalar field theories and the construction of renormalizable gauge theories in the star-product formalism.

The Drinfel’d twist deformation formalism enables the construction of broad classes of noncommutative field theories through natural definitions of star-products, noncommutative differential calculi and deformed symmetries. Those twists which are based on the universal enveloping Hopf algebra of the Poincaré algebra are of particular interest due to their role in integrable deformations of the AdS/CFT correspondence
[
41
,
42
]
and the fact that, among other notable features, they provide a well-defined Hodge dual operation necessary for constructing Yang-Mills theories
[
33
]
. In this paper we will revisit the quantization and UV/IR mixing problem for an example of a Drinfel’d twist deformed scalar field theory from a new perspective. Our approach is rooted in a novel combination of two techniques: the Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) formalism and harmonic analysis.

The most studied examples of noncommutative field theories are defined with the Moyal star-product. They can be obtained via a twist constructed from the generators
P_{\mu}
of translations on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
as

\mathcal{F}_{\text{Moyal}}=\exp\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,P_{\mu}\otimes P_{\nu}\big)\ ,

where
(\theta^{\mu\nu})
is a real
d{\times}d
constant antisymmetric matrix. The Moyal twist defines a noncommutative deformation of Minkowski space
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
and can be used to deform the usual pointwise product of functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
to a star-product
\star
, which leads to the canonical (constant) noncommutativity between coordinate functions

[x^{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}x^{\nu}]:=x^{\mu}\star x^{\nu}-x^{\nu}\star x^{\mu}={\mathrm{i}}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\ .

The angular twist
[
14
]
is a straightforward generalisation of the Moyal twist on
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
using a pair of commuting generators of rotations in a spatial plane
\mathbbm{R}^{2}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{4}
and translations in a normal direction
\mathbbm{R}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{4}
to the plane. For a spatial normal direction the corresponding spacetime is called
\lambda
-Minkowski space and it
represents a noncommutative deformation of Minkowski space
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
in which translational invariance is broken in the plane. Denoting Cartesian coordinates of
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
as
(x^{\mu})=(t,x^{i})=(t,x,y,z)
, where
t
is time, the noncommutative relations among coordinate functions are now

[t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}x^{i}]=0\quad,\quad[z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}x]=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,y\quad,\quad[z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}y]={\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,x\,

for a constant
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
. The closely related
\rho
-Minkowski space interchanges the roles of
t
and
z
, and renames the deformation parameter
\lambda
to
\rho
.

Noncommutative scalar
\varPhi^{4}
-theory based on the angular twist is studied in
[
12
,
27
,
43
]
. Compared to the Moyal deformed
\varPhi^{4}
-theory on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
, noncommutativity improves the ultraviolet behaviour of non-planar diagrams, reducing quadratic divergences to logarithmic divergences. However, UV/IR mixing persists and the theory remains non-renormalizable. An alternative quantization scheme, which is covariant under the
\rho
-Poincaré group, was proposed in
[
20
]
and shown to eliminate UV/IR mixing on
\rho
-Minkowski space. This is reminescent of recent developments which interpret noncommutative field theories as braided field theories
[
11
,
22
]
, whose quantization in the case of Moyal deformed
\varPhi^{4}
-theory
[
13
]
as well as
\varPhi^{3}
-theory
[
7
]
likewise bypasses the UV/IR mixing problem. One of the main goals of the present paper is to develop braided scalar field quantum field theories with the angular twist deformation, as the first example that goes beyond the conventional Moyal twist deformation, and to provide a detailed side-by-side comparison with the more standard noncommutative scalar quantum field theories for the first time.

The construction of noncommutative quantum field theories advocated in
[
35
,
7
]
is based on the modern algebraic approach to the BV formalism due to
[
10
]
. The great virtue of this approach is that it does not introduce a path integral and instead gives an algebraic prescription for calculating correlation functions. This is particularly relevant for noncommutative field theories where it is difficult to consistently define a path integral: among other issues, it is not obvious what the measure on the space of noncommutative fields should be. Although string theory predicts that the standard Feynman measure should be used, this generically leads to the fatal non-renormalizability issues discussed above and moreover is not compatible with the deformed symmetries of noncommutative spacetime.

Braided quantum field theory is based on a braided generalisation of BV quantization, typically (but not exclusively) through a Drinfel’d twist deformation of a classical BV theory. By construction these theories are covariant under a noncocommutative triangular Hopf algebra of symmetries; in particular, the space of noncommutative fields becomes an object in the symmetric monoidal category of representations (modules) of the Hopf algebra. Forgetting the module structures, and hence resorting to the trivial braiding provided by the usual flip map on the category of vector spaces, one recovers the standard Feynman path integral quantization of the noncommutative field theory.

In the case of the Moyal twist this braided BV quantization eliminates non-planar diagrams and hence UV/IR mixing, so that the noncommutative contributions to higher-loop diagrams amount simply to phase factors in external momenta. The resulting noncommutative field theories are thereby renormalizable in the standard textbook sense, but they still fail to provide an ultraviolet completion of quantum field theory. This was first discussed in
[
36
]
from a different algebraic approach to braided quantum field theory, and subsequently rediscovered from various points of view, see e.g.
[
6
,
24
]
. In this paper we explore whether this feature continues to hold beyond the Moyal deformation by constructing braided scalar
\varPhi^{3}
-theory on
\lambda
-Minkowski space.

Compared to the traditional approach, braided BV quantization comes with a proliferation of braiding operators. For the Moyal twist, these operators are diagonalised in the standard basis of plane waves for the decomposition of scalar fields on
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, which also diagonalise the free propagator of the theory. However, this is no longer true for the angular twist, and while the standard noncommutative quantum field theory can be nevertheless studied in this basis as in
[
12
,
27
,
43
]
, in the braided theory the plane wave basis introduces rather severe technical complications and is inconvenient to use. It also violates the covariance requirements of braided BV quantization.

We circumvent this problem in the following way. On general grounds, any abelian Drinfel’d twist can be locally cast in a Moyal-like form via a suitable change of coordinates
[
3
]
; for the angular twist these are cylindrical coordinates on a spatial section
\mathbbm{R}^{3}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{4}
. The special feature of the angular twist is that there exists a basis of cylindrical harmonics in these coordinates that diagonalises both the free propagator and the twist, and consequently all braiding operators. Then all calculations are formally identical to those performed for the Moyal twist, with the caveat that correlation functions are now expressed in terms of radial and angular momenta instead of the more familiar four-momenta in Cartesian coordinates, and involve analytical manipulations with cylindrical Bessel functions. One of the main achievements of this paper is the demonstration of how to reproduce standard features of quantum field theory in this unconventional basis of states. In particular, we illustrate how the usual logarithmic ultraviolet divergences of
\varPhi^{3}
-theory in four dimensions are reproduced in planar diagrams, as well that non-planar diagrams are eliminated and the braided scalar field theory is again free from UV/IR mixing.

To illustrate the versatility of our new approach, we also treat the standard noncommutative
\varPhi^{3}
-theory on
\lambda
-Minkowski space for the first time. While neither the usual (unbraided) BV formalism nor path integral quantization require the basis of cylindrical harmonics, we believe that it is nonetheless more natural and convenient to work with. In particular, we demonstrate how the angular twist deformation generically eliminates logarithmic ultraviolet divergences in non-planar diagrams and renders them
finite
. UV/IR mixing now appears in a much more severe form compared to the Moyal deformation (a similar behaviour was also mentioned in passing for the noncommutative
\varPhi^{4}
-theory in
[
12
]
): in the limit of vanishing angular momentum there is an infinite lattice
\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\,\mathbbm{Z}\subset\mathbbm{R}
of exceptional axial momenta at which the non-planar diagrams exhibit non-analytic behaviour, in that they diverge at these points and the usual logarithmic ultraviolet divergences of the planar diagrams reappear. This makes the quantum field theory much more pathological than usual, because the axial momentum itself is not a periodic variable. We refer to this phenomenon as ‘periodic UV/IR mixing’.

We also quantize the usual BV theory in the conventional plane wave basis and present a detailed comparison with the calculations in the novel basis of cylindrical harmonics. We verify the equivalence of planar contributions to the commutative results (up to symmetry factors and phase factors depending only on external momenta) as in the case of the Moyal twist, which went previously unnoticed in the earlier calculations of
[
12
,
27
,
43
]
, and we recover precisely the same ultraviolet finite non-planar contributions with periodic UV/IR mixing. We demonstrate the equivalence of correlators computed in the two sets of bases by deriving an explicit Fourier series transformation between them, which also elucidates the physical meaning of the deformed momentum conservation laws in the plane wave basis.

Outline

The content in the remainder of this paper is organized as follows:

•

In
Section
2
we review the basics of angular twist differential geometry and solve the Helmholtz equation for the Klein-Gordon operator in cylindrical coordinates. Separation of variables reduces the radial equation to the Bessel equation.
This yields the alternative basis of cylindrical harmonics for the spectral decomposition of noncommutative scalar fields that we use throughout this paper, in addition to the standard plane wave basis.

•

In
Section
3
we review the algebraic BV quantization techniques based on
L_{\infty}
-algebras and homological perturbation theory. In the rest of the paper this method is applied to the
\varPhi^{3}
-model on
\lambda
-Minkowski space. Depending on the choice of corresponding
L_{\infty}
-algebra, we obtain two inequivalent noncommutative quantum field theories: Although the two models have identical classical action, their quantization is different.

•

The braided quantum field theory is discussed in
Section
4
; the natural building blocks are in terms of cylindrical Bessel functions of the first kind. It corresponds to the theory with underlying braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra and explicit noncommutativity given by the braiding determined from the angular twist. Wick’s Theorem is deformed to the Braided Wick Theorem. It gives rise to a renormalizable noncommutative
\varPhi^{3}
-theory. In particular, non-planar diagrams and UV/IR mixing are absent from this model.

•

The standard noncommutative quantum field theory is discussed in
Section
5
. It corresponds to the theory with undeformed (unbraided) underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra and implicit noncommutativity contained in the star-product generated by the angular twist. Wick’s Theorem remains undeformed. It reproduces the standard expectations, a non-renormalizable noncommutative
\varPhi^{3}
-theory with UV/IR mixing in non-planar diagrams, but now in the much more severe form of
periodic
UV/IR mixing. We perform the calculations in both the cylindrical harmonic and plane wave bases, and derive the explicit transformations of correlation functions between the two bases.

•

We conclude with some comments, discussion and outlook for future directions in
Section
6
.

•

Some useful properties of the cylindical Bessel functions of the first kind employed in the calculations of the main text are summarised in
Appendix
A
.

2
Angular twist deformation of Minkowski space

The Drinfel’d twist formalism allows for various noncommutative deformations of Minkowski space and enables the construction of field theories thereon. Noncommutativity breaks the standard Poincaré symmetry and replaces it with a twisted Poincaré symmetry. Twisted Poincaré symmetry for field theories with the canonical Moyal deformation was introduced in
[
45
,
9
]
, and elaborated in detail in
[
4
]
. The
\kappa
-Minkowski space, originally introduced in
[
31
]
as a module over the
\kappa
-deformed Poincaré algebra, can also be realised as a twist deformation.
{\sf U}(1)
gauge theory on
\kappa
-Minkowski space is studied in
[
17
]
, while certain cosmological implications have been explored via a Jordanian twist formalism in
[
8
,
2
]
.

The
\rho
- and
\lambda
-Minkowski spaces, which appear as members of a family with twisted Poincaré symmetries including
\kappa
-Minkowski space
[
32
]
, arise from an angular twist deformation
[
14
]
; quantum field theory and quantum mechanics on these spaces are discussed in
[
12
,
25
,
30
,
27
,
43
]
. In this section we briefly review the deformation leading to the
\lambda
-Minkowski space, and then present a novel framework for studying scalar field theories thereon in terms of cylindrical harmonics.

2.1
The angular Drinfel’d twist

The angular twist deformation was originally defined on four-dimensional Minkowski space
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}

[
14
]
, with signature
(+---)
, and in the following we work in this simplest setting in order to highlight the essential features of our formalism without too much clutter in the formulas. The analysis readily generalizes to arbitrary dimensions for the angular twists discussed in
[
22
, Example 4.27]
. We write global Cartesian coordinates on
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
as
(x^{\mu})=(t,x,y,z)
where
t
is the time coordinate. The corresponding holonomic vector fields along these directions are written as
\partial_{x^{\mu}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
.

The angular twist operator is constructed as a formal power series with coefficients in
U\mathfrak{a}\otimes U\mathfrak{a}
, where
U\mathfrak{a}
is the universal enveloping Hopf algebra of a two-dimensional abelian subalgebra

\mathfrak{a}:=\mathbbm{R}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(2)\ \subset\ \mathfrak{iso}(1,3)

of the Poincaré algebra, regarded as a subalgebra of the Lie algebra of vector fields on
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
. It is given by the bidifferential operator

\mathcal{F}:=\exp\big({-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\varTheta^{AB}\,X_{A}\otimes X_{B}}\big)\ ,

(2.1)

where
A,B\in\{1,2\}
and
\varTheta^{AB}
is the
2{\times}2
constant antisymmetric matrix

\varTheta^{AB}={\small\bigg({\begin{matrix}0&\lambda\\
-\lambda&0\end{matrix}}\bigg)}\normalsize\ ,

with
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
a formal deformation parameter.

The vector fields in (
2.1
) are defined as

X_{1}=\partial_{z}\qquad,\qquad X_{2}=x\,\partial_{y}-y\,\partial_{x}\ .

These vector fields commute,
[X_{1},X_{2}]=0
, and so the twist (
2.1
) belongs to the broad class of abelian Drinfel’d twists. The terminology ‘angular’ refers to the fact that the vector field
X_{2}
generates rotations around the
z
-axis, i.e. it can be represented locally as
X_{2}=\partial_{\varphi}
where
\varphi
is the polar angle in the
(x,y)
-plane.

The twist (
2.1
) deforms the cocommutative Hopf algebra
U\mathfrak{a}
to a noncocommutative Hopf algebra
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
with the twisted coproduct

{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}\circ{\mathsf{\Delta}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}\ ,

where
{\mathsf{\Delta}}
is the primitive coproduct of
U\mathfrak{a}
. It
breaks translational invariance in the
(x,y)
-plane, leading to a deformation of the coproducts of the momentum operators
P_{x^{\mu}}=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{x^{\mu}}
according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\cal F}(P_{t})=P_{t}\otimes 1+1\otimes P_{t}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
&{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\cal F}(P_{z})=P_{z}\otimes 1+1\otimes P_{z}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
&{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\cal F}(P_{x})=P_{x}\otimes\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)+\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)\otimes P_{x}+P_{y}\otimes\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)-\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)\otimes P_{y}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
&{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\cal F}(P_{y})=P_{y}\otimes\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)+\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)\otimes P_{y}-P_{x}\otimes\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)+\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,P_{z}\big)\otimes P_{x}\ .\end{split}

(2.2)

We can now proceed as usual to define a twisted differential geometry
[
4
]
using (
2.1
) and develop noncommutative quantum field theory as in
[
12
]
. The modified coproducts of momentum operators
P_{x}
and
P_{y}
lead to deformed momentum conservation laws.

However, there exists a choice of local frame in which the twist (
2.1
) assumes a translationally invariant Moyal-like form: on general grounds, by Frobenius’ Theorem any abelian twist is locally equivalent to a Moyal twist on a dense open submanifold via a suitable change of local frame adapted to the corresponding foliation
[
3
]
. For the angular twist (
2.1
), these are the local cylindrical coordinates
(x^{\mu})=(t,r,\varphi,z)
, defined for
r\in[0,\infty)
and
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
by
(x,y)=(r\cos\varphi,r\sin\varphi)
, in which it becomes

\mathcal{F}=\exp\big({-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\lambda}{2}\,(\partial_{z}\otimes\partial_{\varphi}-\partial_{\varphi}\otimes\partial_{z})}\big)\ .

(2.3)

The twist (
2.3
) defines the noncommutative star-product of functions
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
as

\displaystyle f\star g:={\sf m}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f\otimes g)=f\cdot g+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\lambda}{2}\bigg)^{n}\ \sum_{k=0}^{n}\,\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!\,(n-k)!}\,\partial_{z}^{n-k}\,\partial_{\varphi}^{k}f\cdot\partial_{\varphi}^{n-k}\,\partial_{z}^{k}g\ ,

(2.4)

where
{\sf m}(f\otimes g)=f\cdot g
is the commutative pointwise product and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})\subseteq C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
is a suitable subspace of smooth integrable complex-valued functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
which is closed under (
2.4
). The star-product makes the space of functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
into a
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-module algebra.
The exterior algebra of differential forms is deformed in a similar manner
[
15
]
.

The noncommutativity of the star-product is controlled by the triangular universal
R
-matrix
{\cal R}=\mathcal{F}^{-2}
whose inverse is given by

{\cal R}^{-1}=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}:=\exp\big(-\mathrm{i}\,\lambda\,(\partial_{z}\otimes\partial_{\varphi}-\partial_{\varphi}\otimes\partial_{z})\big)\ ,

(2.5)

with implicit summation over repeated upper and lower indices understood throughout.
In particular,

\displaystyle f\star g=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(g)\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(f)\ .

In other words, the star-product of functions is
braided commutative
.

The integration of functions over
\mathbbm{R}^{4}
is defined in the usual way. Since the twist is abelian, the integral is cyclic with respect to the star-product (so that it defines a trace on the noncommutative algebra of functions), and the star-product is closed so that one star-product under the integral can always be traded for the pointwise product of functions, that is,

\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ f_{1}\star f_{2}\star\cdots\star f_{n}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ f_{2}\star\cdots\star f_{n}\star f_{1}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ f_{1}\cdot(f_{2}\star\cdots\star f_{n})\ ,

(2.6)

which follows from formal integration by parts.

2.2
Scalar fields on
\lambda
-Minkowski space

In this paper we will analyse in detail the simplest interacting quantum field theory on
\lambda
-Minkowski space. This is the noncommutative real cubic scalar field theory whose classical action is

\displaystyle S_{\rm cl}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\varPhi\,\big(-\square-m^{2}\big)\,\varPhi-\frac{g}{3!}\,\varPhi\star\varPhi\star\varPhi\Big)\ ,

(2.7)

with
\varPhi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
and
m,g\in\mathbbm{R}
, where
\square+m^{2}
is the massive Klein-Gordon operator and the star-product is given by (
2.4
). We will also refer to this as
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory for brevity.

The usual starting point of perturbation theory is to decompose the scalar fields
\varPhi
in a basis which diagonalises the free field theory, i.e. in a basis of eigenfunctions
\mathtt{f}_{k}
of the Klein-Gordon operator

\displaystyle\square\,\mathtt{f}_{k}=-k^{2}\,\mathtt{f}_{k}\ .

(2.8)

This equation is solved by the usual plane waves
\mathtt{f}_{k}=\mathtt{e}_{k}
, with

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{k}(x):={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,k\,\cdot\,x}

(2.9)

in Cartesian coordinates, where
k=(k_{\mu})\in\big(\mathbbm{R}^{4}\big)^{*}
and the dual pairing
k\,\cdot\,x=k_{\mu}\,x^{\mu}
is with respect to the Minkowski metric. Since these states also diagonalise the momentum operators
P_{x^{\mu}}
, with eigenvalues
-k_{\mu}
, they are closely related to the translational invariance of the theory under consideration.

Here we wish to make the following elementary yet crucial point.
Unlike the Moyal twist, because the twist (
2.3
) breaks translational symmetry in the
(x,y)
-plane, it is not diagonalised by the plane wave basis. This makes the interaction of the theory defined by (
2.7
) quite cumbersome and difficult to handle when decomposed in this way
[
12
]
. However, the angular twist does preserve a cylindrical symmetry, and this suggests solving the eigenvalue problem (
2.8
) for the Klein-Gordon operator instead in cylindrical coordinates, in a way that also diagonalises
\mathcal{F}
and hence exploits the symmetries of the interacting theory. This is possible to do using the solution of the three-dimensional Helmholtz equation in cylindrical coordinates, as we now explain.

We start by expressing the Klein-Gordon operator in the local coordinates
(x^{\mu})=(t,r,\varphi,z)
, which reads

\displaystyle\square=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}-\frac{1}{r}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r\,\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right)\ .

We use separation of variables and seek a solution
\mathtt{f}_{k}=\mathtt{c}_{k}
to (
2.8
) in the form

\mathtt{c}_{k}(x)={\tt T}_{k}(t)\,{\tt Z}_{k}(z)\,{\tt R}_{k}(r)\,{\tt\Omega}_{k}(\varphi)\ .

After separating the
t
and
z
dependence, up to overall arbitrary constants we recover the usual plane waves

\displaystyle{\tt T}_{E}(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,E\,t}\qquad,\qquad{\tt Z}_{k_{z}}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,k_{z}\,z}

with
E,k_{z}\in\mathbbm{R}
.

The partial differential equation (
2.8
) then reduces to

\frac{{\tt\Omega}_{k}^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)}{{\tt\Omega}_{k}(\varphi)}+\frac{r}{{\tt R}_{k}(r)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\big(r\,{\tt R}_{k}^{\prime}(r)\big)+r^{2}\,\big(E^{2}-k_{z}^{2}-k^{2}\big)=0\ .

Separating the
\varphi
dependence leads to

{\tt\Omega}_{\ell}(\varphi)={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,\ell\,\varphi}\ .

Since
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
is an angular coordinate, this is single-valued if and only if
\ell\in\mathbbm{Z}
.

With
\varrho:=r\,\sqrt{E^{2}-k_{z}^{2}-k^{2}}
, this reduces the radial equation to the Bessel equation

\varrho^{2}\,{\tt R}_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(\varrho)+\varrho\,{\tt R}_{\ell}^{\prime}(\varrho)+\big(\varrho^{2}-\ell^{2}\big)\,{\tt R}_{\ell}(\varrho)=0\ .

The solutions are the Bessel functions of the first and second kind of integer order
\ell
, also known as cylindrical harmonics or cylindrical Bessel functions, which are respectively given as

{\tt R}_{\ell}(\varrho)=c_{\ell}\,J_{\ell}(\varrho)+d_{\ell}\,N_{\ell}(\varrho)\ ,

where
c_{\ell}
and
d_{\ell}
are arbitrary constants.
Since the Bessel functions of the second kind
N_{\ell}(\varrho)
have a branch point singularity at
\varrho=0
, we set all coefficients
d_{\ell}=0
. Some relevant properties of the Bessel functions of the first kind
J_{\ell}(\varrho)
, which define entire functions on
\mathbbm{C}
by analytic continuation, are reviewed in Appendix
A
.

It follows that the desired diagonalising basis with cylindrical symmetry is given in terms of cylindrical harmonics as

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}_{k}(x)=\mathtt{c}_{(E,\alpha,\ell,k_{z})}(t,r,\varphi,z):=J_{\ell}(\alpha\,r)\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,\ell\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,E\,t}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,k_{z}\,z}\ ,

(2.10)

where the corresponding eigenvalue in (
2.8
) is

\displaystyle k^{2}=E^{2}-\alpha^{2}-k_{z}^{2}\ .

From the on-shell condition
k^{2}=m^{2}
, it follows that the variables
k=(E,\alpha,\ell,k_{z})
have the following physical interpretation:
E\in\mathbbm{R}
is the energy,
k_{z}\in\mathbbm{R}
is the linear momentum in the
z
-direction,
\alpha\in[0,\infty)
is the magnitude of the radial momentum in the
(x,y)
-plane, and
\ell\in\mathbbm{Z}
is the angular momentum about the
z
-axis — an eigenvalue of the operator
P_{\varphi}=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{\varphi}
.

These basis functions also diagonalise the angular twist operator (
2.3
), with eigenvalue a simple Moyal-like phase factor:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,(\mathtt{c}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}_{k^{\prime}})={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,(k_{z}\,\ell^{\prime}-k_{z}^{\prime}\,\ell)}\,(\mathtt{c}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}_{k^{\prime}})\ .

This streamlines calculations enormously in the basis of cylindrical harmonics, as they are formally identical to those using the Moyal twist in the plane wave basis (
2.9
): many known results of the Moyal deformation can now be simply copied over and adapted to the angular twist deformation. We will make extensive use of this simplification in the present paper.

Finally, we write the spectral decomposition of a real scalar field
\varPhi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
in this basis as

\displaystyle\varPhi=\int_{k}\hskip-12.80373pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \phi_{k}\ \mathtt{c}_{k}\ ,

(2.11)

where
\phi_{k}\in\mathbbm{C}
and we use the short-hand notation

\displaystyle\int_{k}\hskip-12.80373pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ :=\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,\frac{{\rm d}E}{2\pi}\ \frac{{\rm d}k_{z}}{2\pi}\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,\alpha\,{\rm d}\alpha\ \frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sum_{\ell\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ .

The reality condition
\varPhi^{*}=\varPhi
implies
\phi_{k}^{*}=(-1)^{\ell}\,\phi_{k^{*}}
, where
k^{*}=(-E,\alpha,-\ell,-k_{z})
. In particular, the resummation of the plane waves (
2.9
) into cylindrical harmonics can be read off from
[
28
, eq. (35)]
and is given by a Fourier series transformation

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{(E,k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,{\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell\tan^{-1}\big(\frac{k_{y}}{k_{x}}\big)}\ \mathtt{c}_{(E\,,\,\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}\,,\,\ell\,,\,k_{z})}\ .

(2.12)

3
Algebraic approach to Batalin-Vilkovisky quantization

This paper is concerned with the perturbative quantum field theory based on the classical action (
2.7
), from the perspective of the modern homotopy algebraic setting of the Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) formalism and homological perturbation theory. In this section we will briefly review the basics of this algebraic approach to BV quantization of general field theories. Detailed definitions and further details about the constructions of this section can be found in
[
35
,
7
]
.

As discussed in
[
11
,
35
,
22
]
, the formalism makes sense in essentially any category of vector spaces endowed with a closed symmetric monoidal structure. For our purposes, there are two relevant categories of vector spaces, which in the subsequent sections will lead to two different quantum field theories that quantize the
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory defined by (
2.7
):

•

The most commonly used category
\mathscr{V}
of complex vector spaces and linear maps has trivial braiding given by simply permuting the factors of tensor products. Within this category, one reproduces the standard path integral approach to quantum field theory, and in particular the standard quantization of noncommutative field theories
[
22
]
, albeit in a purely algebraic fashion without reference to functional integration. We will refer to this as the ‘standard BV formalism’.

•

The representation category
\mathscr{R}
of modules and equivariant maps for the twisted Hopf algebra
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
associated to the angular twist operator (
2.1
) has non-trivial braiding defined in terms of the
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}
. Working in this category leads to braided quantum field theory
[
11
,
35
,
22
]
, which is entirely based on the Drinfel’d twist formalism and has no path integral description. We will refer to this as the ‘braided BV formalism’. It is important to stress that, in this latter approach, the constraints of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-equivariance must be imposed on all objects and maps in order to ensure that all standard homotopical constructions (e.g. the Homological Perturbation Lemma) carry over to this generalised setting; indeed, this is one of the motivations for introducing the basis of cylindrical harmonics in Section
2.2
.

In this section we work in the former category
\mathscr{V}
in order to most clearly illustrate the formalism.

Let
(V,\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\geqslant 1})
be the
L_{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathscr{V}
which organises the dynamics of a classical field theory containing polynomial interactions, with or without gauge symmetries. The graded vector space

V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V^{k}

contains all the fields of the theory as well as the corresponding antifields. We write
\mathcal{A}
for a generic field of
V
and
\mathcal{A}^{+}
for its antifield; if
\mathcal{A}\in V^{k}
for
k\leqslant 1
, then
\mathcal{A}^{+}\in V^{3-k}
. We denote by
|\mathcal{A}|
the degree of a homogeneous element
\mathcal{A}\in V
; if
\mathcal{A}\in V^{k}
, then
|\mathcal{A}|=k
. The multilinear graded antisymmetric
n
-brackets

\mu_{n}:V^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow V[n-2]

obey a sequence of homotopy Jacobi identities, where
V[l]
for
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
is the vector space
V
with its grading shifted according to
(V[l])^{k}=V^{k+l}
.

In the case of a Lagrangian field theory, its
L_{\infty}
-algebra is further equipped with a cyclic graded symmetric non-degenerate pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\longrightarrow\mathbbm{C}[-3]

between fields and their antifields, usually chosen to be an integration over spacetime or a trace. Then the classical action of the theory is captured by the Maurer-Cartan functional

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{\tiny MC}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\mu_{1}(A)\rangle+\sum_{n\geqslant 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\langle A,\mu_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle

on the physical fields
A\in V^{1}
. Its variational equation is the Maurer-Cartan equation of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra, which captures the classical equations of motion of the field theory.

The free field theory is determined by the lowest bracket
\mu_{1}:V\longrightarrow V[-1]
, which is a differential making
(V,\mu_{1})
into a cochain complex. The cohomology
H^{\bullet}(V)
of this complex is the space of classical vacua of the BV theory; it is also a cochain complex with the trivial differential. The complex
(V,\mu_{1})
is quasi-isomorphic to
(H^{\bullet}(V),0)
. For this, we introduce a homotopy equivalence through a degree zero inclusion map
\mathsf{i}\,
, a degree zero projection map
{\sf p}
and a contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
of degree
-1
, which together satisfy the conditions that define a (strong) retract of
(V,\mu_{1})
onto
(H^{\bullet}(V),0)
:

\hbox to137.12pt{\vbox to39.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.5613pt\lower-13.28328pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(3.1)

The map
{\sf h}
is a cochain homotopy equivalence between
\mathrm{id}_{V}
and
\mathsf{i}\circ{\sf p}
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathsf{i}\circ{\sf p}=\mathrm{id}_{V}+\mu_{1}\circ{\sf h}+{\sf h}\circ\mu_{1}\ ,

which is determined by the propagator of the free theory.

Quantization and interactions among fields can be introduced once we extend these data to the space of functionals

\mathrm{Sym}(V[1]^{*})=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\,(V[1]^{*})^{\odot n}

on
V[1]
, which are the classical polynomial observables of the BV theory. This is the unital graded commutative algebra generated by symmetric products of coordinate functionals on
V[1]
, with product denoted

\displaystyle F\odot F^{\prime}=(-1)^{|F|\,|F^{\prime}|}\ F^{\prime}\odot F\ ,

and
(V[1]^{*})^{\odot\,0}:=\mathbbm{C}
playing the role of the constant functionals. Using the non-degenerate pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
we can identify
V[1]^{*}=V[2]
: the duality pairing between
V[2]
and
V[1]
is given by

\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle[3]:V[2]\otimes V[1]=(V\otimes V)[3]\longrightarrow\mathbbm{C}[-3][3]=\mathbbm{C}\ .

Then
\big(\mathrm{Sym}(V[2]),\mu_{1}\big)
is the derived space of observables of the free BV theory, where the differential
\mu_{1}
is extended as a graded derivation of degree zero to the entire algebra
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
.
In this way we obtain a new homotopy equivalence of cochain complexes

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.96643pt}{6.15831pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

(3.2)

The cochain maps
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{I}
and
\mathsf{H}
are the “thickened” extensions of the corresponding cochain maps in (
3.1
).

The data are further supplemented with the BV antibracket which is the graded shifted Poisson bracket

\{-,-\}:\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])[1]

(3.3)

that is compatible with the differential
\mu_{1}
.
It is defined by its non-zero action on generators from
V
as

\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+}\}:=\langle\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+}\rangle\ ,

and extended as a graded derivation on
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
in each of its slots.

Given the homotopy equivalence (
3.2
), we now make a small perturbation of the free BV differential
\mu_{1}
to the differential

Q_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}=\mu_{1}+{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}\ .

Then the Homological Perturbation Lemma constructs perturbed cochain maps
\mathsf{P}_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}
,
\mathsf{I}_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}
and
\mathsf{H}_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}
that define another homotopy equivalence of complexes.
In the category of vector spaces
\mathscr{V}
, the perturbation of the projection
\mathsf{P}_{{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}:\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(H^{\bullet}(V[2]))
implements the path integral via homotopy transfer
[
18
]
.

The (smeared) physical
n
-point correlation functions of the quantum field theory are then defined as
[
35
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{n}(\mathtt{f}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{f}_{n}):=\mathsf{P}_{{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}(\mathtt{f}_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\mathtt{f}_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\,\mathsf{P}\,\big(({{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}\,\mathsf{H})^{m}\,(\mathtt{f}_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\mathtt{f}_{n})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(3.4)

where the external states
\mathtt{f}_{a}\in V^{2}
are test functions corresponding to insertions of the physical fields
A_{a}\in V^{1}
. We are interested in two important perturbations
{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}
:

•

The free quantum field theory is defined by
{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}_{\hbar}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}
with the BV Laplacian

{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}:\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])[1]\ .

The properties of the BV Laplacian follow from the fact that the perturbed differential is again a differential,
Q_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\hbar}}^{2}=(\mu_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}})^{2}=0
, and its definition as a graded derivation on
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
up to the antibracket (
3.3
). Explicitly, it is defined on generators by

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(1)=0\quad,\quad{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(\mathcal{A})=0={\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(\mathcal{A}^{+})\quad,\quad{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(\mathcal{A}\odot\mathcal{A}^{+})=\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+}\}=\langle\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+}\rangle\ ,

and extended to arbitrary elements
F,F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
as

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(F\odot F^{\prime})={\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(F)\odot F^{\prime}+(-1)^{|F|}\,F\odot{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(F^{\prime})+\{F,F^{\prime}\}\ .

The BV Laplacian implements Wick’s Theorem in correlation functions.

•

The interacting field theory is defined by
{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}_{\rm int}=-\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}
is the interacting part of the BV master action, defined as
[
29
]

\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}=\sum_{n\geqslant 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\langle\xi,\mu_{n}^{\rm ext}(\xi^{\otimes n})\rangle^{\rm ext}\ \in\ \mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\ .

(3.5)

Here the contracted coordinate functions
\xi
are degree one elements of
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\otimes V
defined by
\xi=\mathtt{v}^{I}\otimes\mathtt{v}_{I}
, where
\mathtt{v}_{I}
is a basis for
V
with dual basis
\mathtt{v}^{I}=\mathtt{v}_{I}^{+}
with respect to the cyclic pairing. The superscripts
ext
denote the extension of the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-structure on the (shifted) space of BV fields
V
to the tensor product
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\otimes V
with the graded commutative algebra of classical observables
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
.

With the sum
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\hbar}+{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\rm int}
of these two perturbations, which defines the interacting quantum BV differential
Q_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}
, the expression (
3.4
) is a formal power series in Planck’s constant
\hbar
and in the coupling constants appearing in
\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}
with coefficients valued in
\mathrm{Sym}(H^{\bullet}(V[2]))
. That is, the quantum correlator (
3.4
) is a functional on the space of classical vacua. Upon evaluation on a particular vacuum state, this represents the perturbative expansion of the quantum field theory about that vacuum.

The relation between the standard and braided BV formalisms discussed at the beginning of this section can now be clarified through the lens of
A_{\infty}
-algebras. An
A_{\infty}
-structure has no symmetries and so is agnostic to the braiding of the monoidal category. Given an
A_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
\mathscr{R}
, braided graded antisymmetrization of its brackets results in a braided
L_{\infty}
-structure. On the other hand, this same
A_{\infty}
-structure defines an
A_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
\mathscr{V}
under the forgetful functor
\mathscr{R}\longrightarrow\mathscr{V}
which forgets the
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-module structures, and standard graded antisymmetrization of its brackets now leads to an ordinary
L_{\infty}
-structure. In fact, all elements of homological perturbation theory apply to
A_{\infty}
-algebras, and (braided) quantum field theory can be developed equally well in the language of
A_{\infty}
-structures by replacing the (braided) symmetric algebra
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
above with the free unital tensor algebra
{\rm T}(V[2])=\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 0}\,(V[2])^{\otimes n}
over
V[2]
.

4
Braided Batalin-Vilkovisky formalism for
{\boldsymbol{\varPhi_{\star}^{3}}}
-theory

The homotopy algebra underlying the noncommutative cubic scalar field theory defined by (
2.7
) is most straightforwardly constructed by applying the Drinfel’d twist deformation formalism, with the angular twist operator (
2.3
), directly to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra which organises the corresponding commutative theory; the latter is in fact a differential graded Lie algebra (in the category of
L_{\infty}
-algebras). In this way we obtain the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra which completely determines the classical
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory (in the category of braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras). From this perspective, it is more natural to start with the quantization of
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory as a braided quantum field theory. In this section we give an elaborate account of this.

4.1
L_{\infty}
-structure

Consider the twisted
L_{\infty}
-structure
(V,\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}},\mu_{2}^{\mathcal{R}})
with underlying graded vector space

V=V^{1}\oplus V^{2}

consisting of physical fields
\varPhi\in V^{1}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
and their antifields
\varPhi^{+}\in V^{2}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
. The non-vanishing brackets are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(\varPhi)&=\big(-\square-m^{2}\big)\,\varPhi\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})&=g\ \varPhi_{1}\star\varPhi_{2}=\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{2}\big(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\varPhi_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\varPhi_{1})\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.1)

Since the binary bracket
\mu_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}
is braided symmetric, this defines a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

This braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra is strictly cyclic, with cyclic pairing

\langle\varPhi,\varPhi^{+}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \varPhi\star\varPhi^{+}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \varPhi\cdot\varPhi^{+}\ .

(4.2)

The classical action (
2.7
) is then reproduced by the corresponding Maurer-Cartan functional

S_{\rm cl}=\tfrac{1}{2!}\,\langle\varPhi,\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(\varPhi)\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle\varPhi,\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(\varPhi,\varPhi)\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

The cohomology
H^{\bullet}(V)
of the underlying cochain complex
(V,\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}})
describes the classical vacua of the free
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory. It is itself a cochain complex concentrated in degrees one and two, given by the solution space
H^{1}(V)=\ker(\square+m^{2})
of the massive Klein-Gordon equation and the space
H^{2}(V)={\rm coker}(\square+m^{2})
, with the trivial differential
0
.

To set up a retract (
3.1
) which determines a quasi-isomorphism between the complexes
(V,\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}})
and
(H^{\bullet}(V),0)
in the representation category of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-modules
\mathscr{R}
, we need to find a homotopy
{\sf h}:V^{2}\longrightarrow V^{1}
which is a
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-invariant map
[
35
]
. In contrast to the case of the usual Moyal twist, where full translational symmetry is preserved by the noncommutative deformation, here the projection map
{\sf p}:V\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(V)
must be equivariant under the action of translations and rotations with respect to the
z
-direction.

The symmetries respected by the angular twist deformation uniquely select the basis vectors of
V
to be the cylindrical harmonics of Section
2.2
, as the usual plane wave basis (
2.9
) in Cartesian coordinates is incompatible with the
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-equivariance imposed by the twist (
2.3
). In the notation of (
2.10
), we take

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}_{k}(x):\!

\displaystyle=\mathtt{c}_{(E,\alpha,\ell,k_{z})}(t,r,\varphi,z)\ \in\ V^{1}\ ,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}^{k}(x):\!

\displaystyle=\mathtt{c}_{k^{*}}(x)=\mathtt{c}_{(-E,\alpha,-\ell,-k_{z})}(t,r,\varphi,z)\ \in\ V^{2}\ .

(4.4)

Since the cylindrical Bessel functions satisfy the orthogonality relation (
A.2
), the duality between these basis vectors in the spaces of fields and antifields follows as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathtt{c}_{k_{1}}\,,\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}&=\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,{\rm d}t\ {\rm d}z\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,{\rm d}r\ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\,{\rm d}\varphi\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,\ell_{1}\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,E_{1}\,t}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,k_{1z}\,z}\\[-8.0pt]
&\hskip 170.71652pt\times\,J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\ell_{2}\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,E_{2}\,t}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,k_{2z}\,z}\\[4.0pt]
&=(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}-E_{2})\ \delta(k_{1z}-k_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ .\end{split}

(4.5)

Consider now the Poincaré-invariant Feynman propagator

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}=-\frac{1}{\square+m^{2}}\quad,\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)=\frac{1}{k^{2}-m^{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,0^{+}}\ ,

(4.6)

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)
denotes the eigenvalues of the Green operator
\mathsf{G}
when acting on the basis vectors (
4.4
), with
k^{2}=E^{2}-\alpha^{2}-k_{z}^{2}
. The Green operator satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}\circ\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}=-\mathsf{G}\circ\big(\square+m^{2}\big)=\mathrm{id}_{V^{1}}\qquad,\qquad\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}\circ\mathsf{G}=-\big(\square+m^{2}\big)\circ\mathsf{G}=\mathrm{id}_{V^{2}}\ .

The retract conditions of (
3.1
) can now be solved. For example, consider the projection
{\sf p}=0
onto the trivial vacuum state
\varPhi=\varPhi^{+}=0
. Then the cochain homotopy equivalence

{\sf h}:V^{2}\longrightarrow V^{1}
between
\mathrm{id}_{V}
and
\mathsf{i}\circ{\sf p}=0
is given by
{\sf h}=-\mathsf{G}
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle{\sf h}(\varPhi^{+})_{k}=-\frac{1}{k^{2}-m^{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,0^{+}}\,\phi^{+}_{k}=-\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)\,\phi^{+}_{k}\ ,

(4.7)

for
\varPhi^{+}\in V^{2}
. In the following we abbreviate

m_{+}^{2}:=m^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\,0^{+}

inside loop integrals.

4.2
Quantum correlation functions

Since the
L_{\infty}
-algebra organising the classical theory is a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra, i.e. an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the twisted Hopf representation category
\mathscr{R}
, which carries a non-trivial (but symmetric) braiding, all maps defined in Section
3
have to be consistently deformed by the twist (
2.3
) in order to define morphisms in this category. In particular, the algebra of classical observables on
V[1]
is now the unital braided symmetric algebra

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\,(V[2])^{\odot_{\mathcal{R}}n}\ ,

defined via the twisted symmetric product
\odot_{\mathcal{R}}
which is braided graded commutative:

\displaystyle F\odot_{\mathcal{R}}F^{\prime}=(-1)^{|F|\,|F^{\prime}|}\ \mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(F^{\prime})\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(F)\ .

(4.8)

Following the prescription of Section
3
, the cochain maps
{\sf p}
and
{\sf h}
are extended to cochain maps
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{H}
on the noncommutative algebra of classical observables
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
. We are interested in the computation of vacuum correlation functions, which requires restricting to the trivial projection map
{\sf p}=0

[
37
,
13
]
.

To introduce interactions and quantize the theory, we first extend the differential
\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}
as a strict graded derivation of degree zero to the entire algebra
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
. We then perturb this free BV differential to the interacting quantum BV differential

\displaystyle Q_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\mathcal{R}}}=\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}+{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\mathcal{R}}

on
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
, with

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\mathcal{R}}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}-\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},-\}_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

The cochain map
\{-,-\}_{\mathcal{R}}
is the angular twist deformation of the classical antibracket
\{-,-\}
; in particular, it is now braided graded symmetric and defines a braided graded derivation on
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
in each of its slots. The formal definition of the BV Laplacian
{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}
on
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
is unchanged from Section
3
, but it now implements the Braided Wick Theorem in correlation functions; further details can be found in
[
35
,
7
]
.

The correlation functions of the (interacting) braided quantum field theory are written in a momentum space representation analogous to (
3.4
) as

\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\,\mathsf{P}\,\big((-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}-\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},-\}_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mathsf{H})^{m}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\cdots\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{n}})\big)\ ,

(4.9)

where the external states are the antifields
\mathtt{c}^{p_{a}}
defined in (
4.4
) and all momenta
p_{a}=(E_{a},\alpha_{a},\ell_{a},p_{za})
are treated as incoming in the propagators.
Since
{\sf p}=0
, only
\mathsf{P}(1)=1
is non-zero, and hence this is a formal power series expansion in Planck’s constant
\hbar
and the coupling constant
g
.

The definition of the quantum correlator (
4.9
) has the following representation theoretic meaning. Recall that the fields and antifields are naturally superpositions of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-invariant cylindrical harmonics.
By construction, the
n
-point function (
4.9
) is determined by the perturbed projection map

\mathsf{P}_{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\mathcal{R}}}:(V[2])^{\odot_{\mathcal{R}}n}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{C}

from the
n
-th irreducible (braided) symmetric representation of the two-dimensional abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
, with weight
(p_{1z}+\cdots+p_{nz},\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n})
, to the trivial representation
\mathbbm{C}
. This map is
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-equivariant if and only if these weight spaces are themselves isomorphic to the trivial representation, that is,

\displaystyle p_{1z}+\cdots+p_{nz}=0\qquad,\qquad\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}=0\ .

Thus the braided quantum field theory in the cylindrical harmonic basis naturally imposes the kinematical conservation of axial and angular momenta in correlation functions. This is appropriate here since the theory does not exhibit the full translational invariance, as translational symmetry in the
(x,y)
-plane is broken, but rather has a cylindrical symmetry. This would not be true in the standard basis of plane waves (
2.12
), which do not span irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{a}
and instead lead to a deformed momentum conservation law in correlation functions. We will return to this point in Section
5.5
.

4.3
Interacting Batalin-Vilkovisky master action

The interaction functional
\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])
for braided
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory is defined by

\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{{\mathcal{R}}}:=-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle\xi\,,\,\mu^{{\mathcal{R}}\,{\rm ext}}_{2}(\xi,\xi)\rangle^{\rm ext}_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

In the notation of (
2.11
), the contracted coordinate functions are given by

\displaystyle\xi=\int_{k}\hskip-12.80373pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \big(\mathtt{c}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}^{k}+\mathtt{c}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}_{k}\big)\ \in\ \mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])\otimes V\ ,

with the basis functions (
4.3
) and (
4.4
). Crucially,
\xi
is a
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-invariant element of
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{R}}(V[2])\otimes V
.

Repeating the same calculations as in
[
7
]
, after applying all the extended operations using their definitions we get

\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{{\mathcal{R}}}=-\frac{g}{3!}\ \int_{k_{1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{3}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \mathtt{c}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathsf{R}_{\beta}(\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}})\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathsf{R}_{\rho}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}})\ \langle\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\,\mathsf{R}^{\rho}(\mathtt{c}_{k_{1}})\,,\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\mathtt{c}_{k_{2}})\star\mathtt{c}_{k_{3}}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

(4.10)

Evaluating the actions of the
R
-matrices leads to a formally identical result as for the case of the Moyal twist
[
7
]
in terms of phase factors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{{\mathcal{R}}}=-\frac{g}{3!}\ \int_{k_{1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{3}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,(k_{az}\,\ell_{b}-k_{bz}\,\ell_{a})}\ \mathtt{c}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}}\ \langle\mathtt{c}_{k_{1}}\,,\,\mathtt{c}_{k_{2}}\star\mathtt{c}_{k_{3}}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

Explicit calculation of the cyclic pairing gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathtt{c}_{k_{1}}\,,\,\mathtt{c}_{k_{2}}\star\mathtt{c}_{k_{3}}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}&={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,(k_{1z}\,\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}\,k_{2z})}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,((k_{1z}+k_{2z})\,\ell_{3}-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})\,k_{3z})}\\
&\quad\,\times\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,{\rm d}t\ {\rm d}z\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,{\rm d}r\ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\,{\rm d}\varphi\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{3}\,r)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\,t}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,(k_{1z}+k_{2z}+k_{3z})\,z}\\[4.0pt]
&={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,(k_{az}\,\ell_{b}-k_{bz}\,\ell_{a})}\ (2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(k_{1z}+k_{2z}+k_{3z})\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt\times\delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\ \mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

Here the function
\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
is given as an integral of the product of three cylindrical Bessel functions

\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,{\rm d}r\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{3}\,r)\ .

(4.11)

With the constraint
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}=0
, which is satisfied here due to angular momentum conservation, this integral can be computed explicitly
[
5
,
21
,
28
]
. The result is non-zero only when certain conservation laws for the radial momenta are satisfied, as discussed in Appendix
A
where explicit formulas may also be found.

Finally, the interacting BV master action can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{{\mathcal{R}}}&=\int_{k_{1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{3}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ \mathtt{c}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}}\ ,\end{split}

(4.12)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})&=-\frac{g}{3!}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,(k_{az}\,\ell_{b}-k_{bz}\,\ell_{a})}\ (2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(k_{1z}+k_{2z}+k_{3z})\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt\times\delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\ \mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

(4.13)

The vertex (
4.13
) consists of a phase factor, Dirac distributions that are invariant under any permutation of the corresponding momenta {
E_{a},k_{az},\ell_{a}
}, and the integral of cylindrical Bessel functions
\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
which by its definition is trivially invariant under any permutation of the pairs of momenta (
\alpha_{a},\ell_{a}
):

\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3})=\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2})\ .

(4.14)

From these elementary facts it is easy to derive the braided symmetries

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,(k_{1z}\,\ell_{2}-k_{2z}\,\ell_{1})}\ V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{2},k_{1},k_{3})={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,(k_{2z}\,\ell_{3}-k_{3z}\,\ell_{2})}\ V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{3},k_{2})\ ,

(4.15)

and, after applying the delta-functions
\delta(k_{2z}+k_{1z}+k_{3z})
and
\delta_{\ell_{2}+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},0}
, the cyclic symmetry

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{1})\ .

(4.16)

Thus the interaction vertex (
4.13
) is completely analogous to the corresponding Moyal-deformed interaction vertex computed in the usual basis of plane waves
[
7
]
. As a particular consequence, the perturbative expansions of correlation functions may involve both planar and non-planar contributions.

4.4
Lowest multiplicity tree-level functions

In order to illustrate the calculations in the cylindrical harmonic basis and to gain some physical intuition behind this choice of basis, we first compute the non-trivial tree-level contributions to the lowest multiplicity correlation functions (
4.9
).

Two-point function

The tree-level contribution to the two-point function
\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})
is just the free propagator

\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\big[\big({\sf h}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}})\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\big)+\big(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}{\sf h}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\big)\big]\ .

The first term is given by

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\big({\sf h}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}})\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=-(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,{\rm d}r\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ .

Using the orthogonality relation (
A.2
) for cylindrical Bessel functions we obtain

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\big({\sf h}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}})\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=-(2\pi)^{3}\,(-1)^{\ell_{1}}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ .

The contribution from the second term is identical and adding the two contributions results in

\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,(2\pi)^{3}\,(-1)^{\ell_{1}}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ .

(4.17)

In addition to the conservation of axial and angular momenta, the energy and the magnitude of the radial momentum are also conserved, as anticipated.

Three-point function

The tree-level contribution to the three-point function
\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
can be computed starting from

\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}=-({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}^{\mathcal{R}},\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{3}})\big\}_{\mathcal{R}}\ .

(4.18)

Repeating the analogous calculations of
[
7
]
, the one-particle irreducible contributions obtained via the Braided Wick Theorem are of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}_{{\textrm{\tiny 1PI}}}&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\int_{k_{1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{3}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ V_{\mathcal{R}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\langle\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}},\mathsf{G}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}})\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\,\langle\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}},\mathsf{G}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\,\langle\mathtt{c}^{k_{1}},\mathsf{G}(\mathtt{c}^{p_{3}})\rangle_{\mathcal{R}}\\[4.0pt]
&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)\,(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z}+p_{3z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{3})\ (-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,(p_{az}\,\ell_{b}-p_{bz}\,\ell_{a})}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\times\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

(4.19)

According to (
A.4
) and (
A.8
), the integral of three cylindrical Bessel functions reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\begin{cases}\ 0\ ,&\quad\vec{\alpha}_{1},\vec{\alpha}_{2},\vec{\alpha}_{3}\neq\vec{0}\ ,\ \vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2}+\vec{\alpha}_{3}\neq\vec{0}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ \displaystyle(-1)^{\ell_{3}}\,\frac{\cos\big(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon_{2}-\ell_{2}\,\upsilon_{1})}{\pi\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}\sin(\upsilon_{3})}\ ,&\quad\vec{\alpha}_{1},\vec{\alpha}_{2},\vec{\alpha}_{3}\neq\vec{0}\ ,\ \vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2}+\vec{\alpha}_{3}=\vec{0}\ ,\\[12.0pt]
\ \displaystyle\delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ (-1)^{\ell_{3}}\,\frac{\delta(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})}{\alpha_{3}}\ ,&\quad\vec{\alpha}_{1}=\vec{0}\ .\end{cases}\end{split}

(4.20)

Thus there are special regions in momentum space where the interactions are non-trivial only when the conservation of momentum is fully restored:
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}=0
. These are the triangular regions where all two-dimensional vectors
\vec{\alpha}_{i}
are non-zero and the radial momentum is conserved, as in (
A.6
); the triangle identities of Appendix
A
ensure the symmetries (
4.14
) even though they are not manifest in (
4.20
). Otherwise, when
\vec{\alpha}_{i}=\vec{0}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, only the magnitude of the radial momentum is conserved.

The final result for (
4.19
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}_{{\textrm{\tiny 1PI}}}&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)\,(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z}+p_{3z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{3})\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,(p_{1z}\,\ell_{2}-p_{2z}\,\ell_{1})}\ \mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

(4.21)

The noncommutative contribution simply appears in the form of a phase factor.

4.5
Propagator corrections at one-loop

The one-loop contribution to the two-point function
\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}&={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\bigr\}_{\mathcal{R}}\big)\bigr\}_{\mathcal{R}}\\
&\quad\,+({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\mathcal{R}}\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\bigr\}_{\mathcal{R}}\bigr\}_{\mathcal{R}}\ .\end{split}

(4.22)

The explicit calculations repeat those of
[
7
]
exactly, where a diagrammatic calculus was developed. Applying this, the complete result for the one-loop two-point function is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}={9\times{\footnotesize\hbox to80.78pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.94402pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(4.23)

Each of the diagrammatic contributions in (
4.23
) can be treated individually, using the explicit formulas from
[
7
]
. We will discard the tadpole contributions; they can be eliminated by curving the
L_{\infty}
-structure as explained in
[
7
]
.
In the following we label the components of internal loop momenta explicitly by their Latin symbol for clarity, whereas external momentum components will remain without this designation.

One-particle irreducible contribution.

Consider the contribution to (
4.23
) from the one-particle irreducible diagram

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip-8.53581pt{\footnotesize{\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

&\hskip-8.53581pt\quad=\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\,(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip-8.53581pt\hskip 28.45274pt\times\ \int_{q_{1}}\hskip-16.5026pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(q_{1})\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{2}}\ \frac{\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{q_{2}},\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{1})\,\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{2},-\ell_{1}-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{2}})}{\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(E_{1}+E_{q_{1}})^{2}+(p_{1z}+q_{1z})^{2}+m_{+}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

(4.24)

The evaluation of the loop integrals in (
4.24
) is subtle due to the discontinuous behaviour of (
4.20
). We assume that
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
and restrict the radial loop momentum integrals to the open intervals
\alpha_{q_{i}}\in(0,\infty)
.
The integrals of Bessel functions then give the contributions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{q_{2}},\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{1})&=(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}}}\,\frac{\cos(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon_{q_{1}}-\ell_{q_{1}}\,\upsilon_{1})}{\pi\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\sin(\upsilon_{q_{2}})}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},\ell_{1},-\ell_{1}-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{2}})&=(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}}}\,\frac{\cos(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon^{\prime}_{q_{1}}-\ell_{q_{1}}\,\upsilon_{2})}{\pi\,\alpha_{2}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\sin(\upsilon^{\prime}_{q_{2}})}\ .\end{split}

(4.25)

We start by evaluating the sum over
\ell_{q_{1}}
using Euler’s formula to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sum_{\ell_{q_{1}}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,\cos(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon_{q_{1}}-\ell_{q_{1}}\,\upsilon_{1})\cos(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon^{\prime}_{q_{1}}-\ell_{q_{1}}\,\upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\delta(\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2})\cos\big(\ell_{1}\,(\upsilon_{q_{1}}-\upsilon_{q_{1}}^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\delta(\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2})\cos\big(\ell_{1}\,(\upsilon_{q_{1}}+\upsilon_{q_{1}}^{\prime})\big)\Big)\ .

Since by definition
\upsilon_{i}
are interior angles of a triangle and cannot be negative, the second Dirac distribution can be discarded. For the first Dirac distribution, we exploit triangle relations (
A.4
) to swap angles with sides. The angles
\upsilon_{1}
and
\upsilon_{2}
are monotonic functions of the radial momenta
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, respectively. This implies that
\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2}
can be treated as a function of
\alpha_{1}
, and
\upsilon_{1}=\upsilon_{2}
if and only if
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.

The cosine law applied to the first momentum triangle in (
4.25
) gives

\cos(\upsilon_{1})=\frac{\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-\alpha^{2}_{1}}{2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}}\ .

Taking the derivative with respect to
\alpha_{1}
gives

\sin(\upsilon_{1})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\upsilon_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}}=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{q_{1}}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}}\ .

The expected radial momentum conservation law now follows as

\delta(\upsilon_{1}-\upsilon_{2})=\frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\frac{\mathrm{d}\upsilon_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}}}=\alpha_{q_{1}}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}\sin(\upsilon_{1})\,\frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ .

(4.26)

There is an additional consequence of (
4.26
): both momentum triangles in (
4.25
) now have equal sides, and therefore they have equal angles, i.e.
\upsilon^{\prime}_{q_{i}}=\upsilon_{q_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.

Applying sine and cosine laws we may now rewrite (
4.24
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\footnotesize{\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.84 0 L -49.21 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

&\quad=\frac{4\pi}{3}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,\frac{{\rm d}E_{q_{1}}}{2\pi}\ \frac{{\rm d}q_{1z}}{2\pi}\ \int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{1}}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(q_{1})\\
&\hskip 36.98866pt\times\int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\frac{\alpha_{q_{2}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{2}}}{\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(E_{1}+E_{q_{1}})^{2}+(p_{1z}+q_{1z})^{2}+m_{+}^{2}}\ \frac{2}{\sqrt{4\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+\alpha^{2}_{1}-\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}})^{2}}}\ .\end{split}

(4.27)

Ultraviolet behaviour.

Let us now discuss the ultraviolet behaviour of the one-particle irreducible two-point function at one-loop order (
4.27
). It is well-known that the corresponding commutative result is logarithmically ultraviolet divergent when computed in the standard plane wave basis. As there are no noncommutative contributions in (
4.27
), it should also exhibit some remnant of this ultraviolet divergence, as such infinities cannot be removable by simply passing to another basis for the spectral decomposition of the scalar fields. In the following we show how the standard ultraviolet divergences are exhibited in the cylindrical harmonic basis.

The internal linear momentum along the
z
-direction and radial momentum are not on equal footing, as a consequence of the breaking of translational symmetry in the
(x,y)
-plane. Hence we cannot apply the standard method of Wick rotating the time direction and changing variables to hyperspherical coordinates in momentum space, in which the radial coordinate corresponds to the magnitude of the total momentum. Instead, we analyse the ultraviolet behaviour by combining the axial momentum with the energy, and then incorporating the radial momentum.

We set
R=(E,p_{z})
and start from the integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,\frac{{\rm d}E_{q_{1}}\,{\rm d}q_{1z}}{\big(\alpha_{q_{1}}^{2}-E_{q_{1}}^{2}+q_{1z}^{2}+m_{+}^{2}\big)\big(\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(E_{1}+E_{q_{1}})^{2}+(p_{1z}+q_{1z})^{2}+m_{+}^{2}\big)}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{2}}\,\frac{{\rm d}^{2}R_{q_{1}}}{\big(R_{q_{1}}^{2}-\alpha_{q_{1}}^{2}-m_{+}^{2}\big)\,\big(({R}_{1}+{R}_{q_{1}})^{2}-\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-m_{+}^{2}\big)}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}x\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{2}}\,\frac{{\rm d}^{2}R_{q_{1}}}{\big(R_{q_{1}}^{2}+2\,(1-x)\,{R}_{q_{1}}\cdot{R}_{1}-m_{+}^{2}-x\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}-(1-x)\,(\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-R_{1}^{2})\big)^{2}}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt={\mathrm{i}}\,\pi\,\int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}x\ \frac{1}{x\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+(1-x)\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}+m_{+}^{2}-x\,(1-x)\,R_{1}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

(4.28)

In the second equality we introduced the standard Feynman parametrisation, while
the last equality is obtained using the table of
D
-dimensional integrals in Minkowski space from
[
38
, eq. (11A)]
for
D=2
.

The final expression for the irreducible contribution is then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\footnotesize{\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 9.84 0 C 9.84 5.44 5.44 9.84 0 9.84 C -5.44 9.84 -9.84 5.44 -9.84 0 C -9.84 -5.44 -5.44 -9.84 0 -9.84 C 5.44 -9.84 9.84 -5.44 9.84 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{{}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\normalsize}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{3}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\,\int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{1}}\ \int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{2}}\ \frac{2}{\sqrt{4\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+\alpha^{2}_{1}-\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}})^{2}}}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\int_{0}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}x\ \frac{1}{x\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+(1-x)\,\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}+m_{+}^{2}-x\,(1-x)\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}\ .\end{split}

(4.29)

The integrations in (
4.29
) are symmetric under exchange
\alpha_{q_{1}}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{q_{2}}
together with
x\rightarrow 1-x
. The integral over
\alpha_{q_{1}}
can now be performed. The full analysis is somewhat technically involved and not very informative, so we will consider a simplifying instance.

In the special case where one of the external momenta is purely axial, say
\alpha_{1}=0
, the radial integrations in (
4.29
) can be made explicit, prior to introducing the Feynman parametrisation. When
\alpha_{1}=0
the first integral of Bessel functions in (
4.25
) reduces according to (
4.20
) as

\displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{q_{2}},\alpha_{q_{1}},0)=\delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ (-1)^{\ell_{q_{1}}}\,\frac{\delta(\alpha_{q_{1}}-\alpha_{q_{2}})}{\alpha_{q_{1}}}\ .

The second integral of Bessel functions in (
4.25
) can be summed over
\ell_{q_{1}}\in\mathbbm{Z}
, which leads to the conservation of radial momenta in the same manner as in (
4.26
).
Altogether this results in

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hskip-8.53581pt{\footnotesize{\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The integration over
\alpha_{q_{1}}\in(0,\Lambda]
may now be performed explicitly, where
\Lambda\to\infty
is an ultraviolet cutoff, and subsequently over
x\in[0,1]
. The final result is
