\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\normalsize}\ \Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{2}}\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ \delta_{\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\times\Bigg(\log\bigg(\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}+1\bigg)+\sqrt{\frac{4\,(\Lambda^{2}+m^{2})}{E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}}-1}\ \tan^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}}{4\,(\Lambda^{2}+m^{2})-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}}\\
&\hskip 184.9429pt-\sqrt{\frac{4m^{2}}{E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}}-1}\ \tan^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}}{4m^{2}-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}}\ \Bigg)\ ,\end{split}

(4.30)

where we use the representation of the arctangent function

\tan^{-1}(w)=\frac{1}{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log\bigg(\frac{1+{\mathrm{i}}\,w}{1-{\mathrm{i}}\,w}\bigg)

which is valid for
w\in\mathbbm{C}\setminus\{\pm\,{\mathrm{i}}\}.

The expression (
4.30
) exhibits two important properties. Firstly, we observe that the Dirac distribution in the external radial momentum exactly fixes
\alpha_{2}=0
, as expected by momentum conservation and since
\alpha_{1}=0
here; as also anticipated both external angular momenta vanish:
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=0
. Secondly, in (
4.30
) we immediately recognize the logarithmic ultraviolet divergence characteristic of the commutative
\varPhi^{3}
-theory in four dimensions.

Standard one-loop correction.

To compare (
4.30
) with its commutative counterpart, and in particular to show that it reproduces the same logarithmic ultraviolet divergence, we recalculate the standard one-loop correction to the propagator obtained in the plane wave basis via the usual Feynman rules. It is given by

&\hskip-8.53581pt\hskip 227.62204pt\times\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{{\rm d}^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{1}{q^{2}-m_{+}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{(p_{1}-q)^{2}-m_{+}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

(4.32)

Changing to cylindrical coordinates in momentum space results in the four-dimensional Dirac distribution

\delta(p_{1}+p_{2})=\delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}-\pi)\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ ,

where
\vartheta_{i}
are polar angles in the
(x,y)
-plane of momentum space (see Appendix
A
).
The denominator in (
4.32
) can be cast into the form

\big(x\,q^{2}+(1-x)\,(p_{1}-q)^{2}-m_{+}^{2}\big)^{2}=\big({R}_{q}^{2}-2\,(1-x)\,{R}_{1}\cdot{R}_{q}-\alpha_{q}^{2}-m_{+}^{2}+(1-x)\,(R_{1}^{2}-\alpha_{1}^{2})\big)^{2}

where
{R_{q}}=(E_{q},q_{z})
and
{R_{1}}=(E_{1},p_{1z})
. The integral over
{R}_{q}\in\mathbbm{R}^{2}
can be easily performed, as shown in (
4.28
). Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\footnotesize{\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to17.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 9.84 0 C 9.84 5.44 5.44 9.84 0 9.84 C -5.44 9.84 -9.84 5.44 -9.84 0 C -9.84 -5.44 -5.44 -9.84 0 -9.84 C 5.44 -9.84 9.84 -5.44 9.84 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{{}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\normalsize}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}-\pi)\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\ \int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}x\ \log\bigg(\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{m^{2}+(1-x)\,\alpha_{1}^{2}-x\,(1-x)\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}+1\bigg)\ .\end{split}

(4.34)

After integration over
x\in[0,1]
, the correction (
4.34
) at
\alpha_{1}=0
is the same as (
4.30
), confirming that the standard logarithmic divergence is reproduced by our braided one-loop two-point function evaluated in the basis of Bessel functions.

Absence of non-planar contributions.

The calculations of this section, going back to the basic form (
4.24
), show that there is no noncommutative contribution to the two-point function (
4.22
). The braided vertex (
4.13
) and the Braided Wick Theorem combine in such a way as to cancel all non-planar contributions, leaving the same result as the corresponding calculations in the undeformed theory (performed in the basis of cylindrical harmonics), up to phase factors which depend only on the momenta of external states. This seems to be a general feature of braided BV-quantized field theories in the absence of non-abelian gauge symmetries
[
35
,
13
,
7
,
16
]
.

5
Standard Batalin-Vilkovisky formalism for
{\boldsymbol{\varPhi_{\star}^{3}}}
-theory

The braided
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
quantum field theory of Section
4
was constructed by applying the twist deformation to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra of the corresponding commutative theory, thus intrinsically introducing noncommutativity. However, this is not the only possible noncommutative deformation of the homotopy algebra organising the commutative model
[
22
]
. In the following we present another version based on an ordinary (unbraided)
L_{\infty}
-algebra, which leads to the standard noncommutative
\varPhi^{3}_{\star}
quantum field theory. Following the discussion at the end of Section
3
, one can regard (
4.1
) as the brackets of an
A_{\infty}
-algebra (in fact a differential graded noncommutative algebra) in the category of vector spaces
\mathscr{V}
, whose graded antisymmetrization in
\mathscr{V}
defines an ordinary
L_{\infty}
-structure. The computation of quantum correlation functions of the corresponding commutative theory directly in terms of this
A_{\infty}
-structure is discussed in
[
37
]
.

Standard noncommutative scalar quantum field theories have been discussed in e.g.
[
34
,
19
,
39
]
for the Moyal deformation and in
[
12
,
27
]
for the case of
\lambda
-Minkowski space, using the conventional basis of plane waves and Feynman quantization. These theories involve both planar and non-planar contributions to one-loop correlation functions, and UV/IR mixing is present. In the following we present the details of the algebraic BV formalism that reproduces the same quantitative results.

5.1
L_{\infty}
-structure

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
corresponding to the standard noncommutative theory again consists of the graded vector space

V=V^{1}\oplus V^{2}

with physical fields
\varPhi\in V^{1}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
and their antifields
\varPhi^{+}\in V^{2}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{4})
. The non-vanishing brackets are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{1}(\varPhi)&=\big(-\square-m^{2}\big)\,\varPhi\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mu_{2}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,g\ (\varPhi_{1}\star\varPhi_{2}+\varPhi_{2}\star\varPhi_{1})=\mu_{2}(\varPhi_{2},\varPhi_{1})\ .\end{split}

(5.1)

Compared to the braided
L_{\infty}
-structure (
4.1
), the binary bracket
\mu_{2}
is strictly symmetric, and so (
5.1
) defines a strict
L_{\infty}
-structure in the category of vector spaces
\mathscr{V}
. Noncommutativity is now implicitly introduced through the symmetrization of the star-product in (
5.1
), rather than explicitly through a braiding as before.

This
L_{\infty}
-algebra is strictly cyclic, with the same cyclic pairing (
4.2
):

\langle\varPhi,\varPhi^{+}\rangle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \varPhi\cdot\varPhi^{+}\ .

(5.2)

Since
\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mu_{2}(\varPhi,\varPhi)=\mu_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(\varPhi,\varPhi)
, the Maurer-Cartan functional coincides again with the classical action (
2.7
):

S_{\rm cl}=\tfrac{1}{2!}\,\langle\varPhi,\mu_{1}(\varPhi)\rangle-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle\varPhi,\mu_{2}(\varPhi,\varPhi)\rangle\ .

In particular, the cohomology
H^{\bullet}(V)
of the cochain complex
(V,\mu_{1})
is also the same as before.

Unlike the braided theory discussed in Section
4
, in this setting there are no restrictions of equivariance on the projection map or the contracting homotopy in
\mathscr{V}
, and therefore no preferred basis of fields or coordinate frame to work in. Nevertheless, we continue to operate in the basis of cylindrical harmonics in order to facilitate comparison with the results and calculations of Section
4
, and also because we believe the perturbative calculations are clearer and more natural in this basis of states. Later on we will compare to the analogous calculations in the more traditional plane wave basis.

In particular, we use the same homotopy equivalence (
3.1
) as before, which now leads to a quasi-isomorphism between the cochain complexes
(V,\mu_{1})
and
(H^{\bullet}(V),0)
in the category
\mathscr{V}
. Since we shall again calculate vacuum correlation functions, we restrict to the trivial projection map
{\sf p}=0
. The homotopy
{\sf h}:V^{2}\longrightarrow V^{1}
is then given by
{\sf h}=-\mathsf{G}
, i.e. by the same formal expression (
4.7
) using the Feynman propagator (
4.6
).

5.2
Quantum correlation functions

Lifting the
L_{\infty}
-structure (
5.1
) to the strictly graded commutative algebra of functionals
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
on
V
no longer involves the non-trivial braiding
{\mathcal{R}}
and we can proceed in a straightforward fashion by directly applying the formulas from Section
3
.
Then the interacting quantum field theory is defined by the perturbation

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\tt cyl}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}-\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},-\}\ .

The correlation functions are now given by

\widetilde{C}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\,\mathsf{P}\,\big((-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}-\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},-\}\,\mathsf{H})^{m}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot\mathtt{c}^{p_{n}})\big)\ ,

(5.3)

with the same basis of antifields
\mathtt{c}^{p}
from (
4.4
) as external states.
In the case of the free quantum field theory, i.e.
\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int}=0
in (
5.3
), this results in the standard (unbraided) Wick Theorem.

5.3
Interacting Batalin-Vilkovisky master action

The interaction functional
\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int}\in\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
is defined by

\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int}:=-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle\xi\,,\,\mu^{{\rm ext}}_{2}(\xi,\xi)\rangle^{\rm ext}\ .

(5.4)

The contracted coordinate functions are again given by the same formula

\displaystyle\xi=\int_{k}\hskip-12.80373pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \big(\mathtt{c}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}^{k}+\mathtt{c}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{c}_{k}\big)\ ,

but now regarded as degree one elements of
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\otimes V
.
The computation of (
5.4
) is formally the same as the computation in Section
4.3
except that there are no
R
-matrices appearing now, and the only noncommutative contributions come from the explicit star-products appearing in (
5.1
).

Symmetrization in the algebra of classical observables
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
, together with the fact that the binary bracket
\mu_{2}
from (
5.1
) is symmetric, results in

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int}=\int_{k_{1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \int_{k_{3}}\hskip-17.07164pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ V_{\tt cyl}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ \mathtt{c}^{k_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}}\ ,

(5.5)

with the vertex

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\tt cyl}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})&=-\frac{g}{3!}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,(k_{az}\,\ell_{b}-k_{bz}\,\ell_{a})}\ (2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(k_{1z}+k_{2z}+k_{3z})\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt\times\delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\ \mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

(5.6)

Up to the signs of the phase factors, the vertex (
5.6
) is identical to the vertex (
4.13
) of the braided theory. The difference in signs owes to the absence of
R
-matrix contributions in the present case.

5.4
Two-point function at one-loop

Let us now analyse in detail the two-point function
\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})
at one-loop order following from (
5.3
) and the analogous calculations of Section
4
.

Tree-level contribution

The tree-level contribution is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}&=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\\[4.0pt]
&={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,(2\pi)^{3}\,(-1)^{\ell_{1}}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \frac{\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}{\alpha_{1}}\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ .\end{split}

(5.7)

As expected, since the braided and standard free theories are the same, the tree-level two-point function (
5.7
) is identical to (
4.17
).

One-loop corrections

The one-loop contribution to the two-point function follows from

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}&={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\bigr\}\big)\bigr\}\\[4.0pt]
&\quad\,+({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\bigl\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt cyl}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})\bigr\}\bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(5.8)

The two terms in (
5.8
) include all possible contractions between the external momenta
(p_{1},p_{2})
and internal momenta coming from a pair of vertices
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
and
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})
. Keeping in mind that we work in the graded commutative algebra
\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
, i.e. there are no
R
-matrices that appear when elements in a symmetrized product are permuted, the standard Wick Theorem applies.

Let us summarise the different types of diagrammatic contributions to
\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}
:

•

Contraction of the external momenta

{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}})

results in disconnected diagrams that consist of the free propagator diagram and two different vacuum diagrams:

{\footnotesize\hbox to85.06pt{\vbox to10.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.52733pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-39.86092pt}{4.42197pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}

{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 29.53 0 L -29.53 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\normalsize\ \right)

(5.9)

•

Contractions between momenta from the same vertex

{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{k_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}})

result in one reducible diagram and two disconnected diagrams of the form

p_{1}
p_{2}

or

p_{1}
p_{2}

or

p_{1}
p_{2}

(5.10)

•

Mixed contractions without permutations of momenta in the vertices

{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{k_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{3}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{2}})

(5.11)

result in the planar one-loop contribution to the one-particle irreducible two-point function

p_{1}
p_{2}

•

Mixed contractions with a single transposition of momenta in one vertex (e.g.
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\to(q_{1},q_{3},q_{2})
)

{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{k_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{p_{2}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{k_{2}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{2}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{c}^{k_{3}}\odot\mathtt{c}^{q_{3}})

(5.12)

introduce a single (trivial) braiding, resulting in the non-planar one-loop contribution to the one-particle irreducible two-point function

p_{1}
p_{2}

We discard the disconnected and reducible diagrams (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) as before, focusing only on the planar and non-planar contributions to the one-particle irreducible two-point function at one-loop.

Planar contribution.

In the notation of Section
4.5
, the explicit form of a typical contribution from the planar diagram is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip-2.84526pt\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{planar}}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip-8.53581pt\quad=\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\,(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip-8.53581pt\hskip 28.45274pt\times\ \int_{q_{1}}\hskip-16.5026pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(q_{1})\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{2}}\ \frac{\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{q_{2}},\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{1})\,\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{2},-\ell_{1}-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{2}})}{\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(E_{1}+E_{q_{1}})^{2}+(p_{1z}+q_{1z})^{2}+m_{+}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

(5.13)

Up to a symmetry factor, this diagram gives exactly the same (commutative) result as the one-particle irreducible diagram obtained from the braided BV formalism in (
4.24
). In particular, it is logarithmically ultraviolet divergent.

Non-planar contribution.

A typical contribution from the non-planar diagram is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\\[4.0pt]
&\quad=\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\,(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\ \int_{q_{1}}\hskip-16.5026pt\mbox{$\sum$}\ \ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,-{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,(p_{1z}\,\ell_{q_{1}}-q_{1z}\,\ell_{1})}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(q_{1})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{2}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{2}}\ \frac{\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{q_{2}},\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{1})\,\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},-\ell_{2},-\ell_{1}-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{2}})}{\alpha^{2}_{q_{2}}-(E_{1}+E_{q_{1}})^{2}+(p_{1z}+q_{1z})^{2}+m_{+}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

(5.14)

This diagram leads to an obvious noncommutative contribution in the form of a phase factor mixing the internal and external momenta; in the braided theory, this factor is canceled by non-trivial
R
-matrix contributions from the Braided Wick Theorem, effectively reducing the non-planar diagram to the planar diagram. As expected, in the
\lambda=0
theory the contribution (
5.14
) reduces to the planar contribution (
5.13
) and hence to the commutative result (up to a symmetry factor).

Periodic UV/IR mixing.

Let us now explore the limit of the two-point function (
5.14
) in which the phase factor tends to unity when
\lambda\neq 0
. In the cylindrical harmonic basis, this occurs as the limit of external momentum in which the angular momentum vanishes,
\ell_{1}\to 0
, and the axial momentum becomes
exceptional
, i.e.
p_{1z}
approaches one of the infinitely many isolated points with
\lambda\,p_{1z}\in 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
and
\sin(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\to 0
. In this ‘periodic’ infrared limit, exceptional momenta render the phase factor ineffective and the whole non-planar correlator (
5.14
) reduces to the planar correlator (
5.13
). This is a potential signal of a periodic form of UV/IR mixing, in that the original ultraviolet divergence reappears as a periodic infrared divergence.

To substantiate this claim, as in Section
4.5
we focus on the special case where the momentum is purely axial, that is,
\alpha_{1}=0
and
\lambda\,p_{1z}\notin 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
. The triple Bessel integrals are completely symmetric under any permutation of pairs
(\alpha_{i},\ell_{i})
and we can sum over
\ell_{q_{1}}\rightarrow-\ell_{q_{1}}
.
From (
A.7
) it is clear that the phase factor in (
5.14
) will not affect the Feynman parametrisation introduced earlier in (
4.28
) and we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ \delta_{\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\sum_{\ell_{q_{1}}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ \int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{1}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,p_{1z}\,\ell_{q_{1}}}\ \int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}x\ \frac{(-1)^{\ell_{q_{1}}}\,\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},0,-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{1}})}{\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+m_{+}^{2}-x\,(1-x)\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}\ .\end{split}

(5.15)

The second triple Bessel integral in (
5.15
) is given by

\mathtt{F}_{\ell_{q_{1}},0,-\ell_{q_{1}}}(\alpha_{q_{1}},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{q_{1}})=(-1)^{\ell_{q_{1}}}\,\frac{\cos(\ell_{q_{1}}\,\upsilon_{2})}{\pi\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,\alpha_{2}\cos(\frac{\upsilon_{2}}{2})}\ ,

and the corresponding momentum triangle is isosceles. The sum over
\ell_{q_{1}}\in\mathbbm{Z}
results in a pair of Dirac distributions

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{q_{1}}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda\,p_{1z}\,\ell_{q_{1}}}\cos(\ell_{q_{1}}\upsilon_{2})=\pi\,\delta(\upsilon_{2}\,+\lambda\,p_{1z})+\pi\,\delta(\upsilon_{2}\,-\lambda\,p_{1z})\ .

As in Section
4.5
, the two Dirac distributions cannot be simultaneously non-zero, and which one can be discarded depends on the sign of
\lambda\,p_{1z}
. The non-vanishing distribution in the angles can then be expressed as a distribution in the sides of the triangle with the aid of the triangle constraint

\sin\Big(\frac{\upsilon_{2}}{2}\Big)=\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}}\ .

To do this, we treat
\delta(\sin(\frac{1}{2}\,(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z}))
as a function of
\upsilon_{2}
and use
\delta(\sin(\upsilon))=\delta(\upsilon)
for
\upsilon\in[0,\pi)
to get

\delta\Big(\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z})\big)\Big)=\delta\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z})\big)=2\,\delta(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z})\ .

(5.16)

On the other hand,
\delta(\sin(\frac{1}{2}\,(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z}))
can also be understood as a function of
\alpha_{2}
, which yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta\Big(\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z})\big)\Big)&=\delta\big(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\,\upsilon_{2})\cos(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\mp\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\,\upsilon_{2})\sin(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\delta\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}}\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\mp\sqrt{1-\Big(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}}\Big)^{2}}\,\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\bigg)\\[4.0pt]
&=2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\cos(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,\delta\big(\alpha_{2}\mp 2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\sin(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.17)

Equating (
5.16
) and (
5.17
) we find

\delta(\upsilon_{2}\mp\lambda\,p_{1z})=\alpha_{q_{1}}\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\,\delta\big(\alpha_{2}\mp 2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\sin(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\big)\ ,

where the sign
\mp
corresponds respectively to
{\rm sgn}(\lambda\,p_{1z})=\pm 1
.

Inserting this result into (
5.15
) gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ \delta_{\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\int_{0^{+}}^{\infty}\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\,{\rm d}\alpha_{q_{1}}\ \int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}x\ \frac{\delta\big(\alpha_{2}\mp 2\,\alpha_{q_{1}}\sin(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\big)}{\alpha_{2}\,\big(\alpha^{2}_{q_{1}}+m_{+}^{2}-x\,(1-x)\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})\big)}\ .\end{split}

(5.18)

Performing the integration over
\alpha_{q_{1}}\in(0,\infty)
and subsequently over
x\in[0,1]
finally leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{12}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \delta_{\ell_{1},0}\ \delta_{\ell_{2},0}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\frac{\displaystyle\tan^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\sin^{2}(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}{\alpha_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,\big(4m^{2}-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})\big)}}}{\big|\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\big|\,\sqrt{\big(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}\big)\,\big(\alpha_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,\big(4m^{2}-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})\big)\big)}}\ .\end{split}

(5.19)

At any non-exceptional momentum
\lambda\,p_{1z}\notin 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
, the non-planar contribution (
5.19
) is finite, in contrast to its logarithmically divergent planar counterpart, and the radial momentum
\alpha_{2}
is no longer conserved thanks to the breaking of translational invariance by noncommutativity. However, taking the limit
\lambda\,p_{1z}\in 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
in (
5.18
) removes the radial momentum
\alpha_{q_{1}}
from the Dirac distribution and leaves
\alpha_{2}
trivially conserved. Thus in either the commutative or periodic infrared limit we completely recover the planar contribution (
4.30
) and its logarithmic ultraviolet divergence. Because of this non-analytic behaviour of (
5.19
) as a function of
\lambda\,p_{1z}
, we conclude that periodic UV/IR mixing is present in the standard BV-quantized
\varPhi^{3}_{\star}
-theory represented in the basis of cylindrical harmonic states.

5.5
Correlation functions in the plane wave basis

As already discussed, in contrast to the braided BV formalism from Section
4
, in the standard BV formalism there are no representation theoretic restrictions needed to define the noncommutative quantum field theory. From this perspective, instead of the cylindrical harmonic basis (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) of fields and antifields, we could just as well appeal to the more conventional Poincaré invariant basis (
2.9
) of plane waves

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=\mathtt{e}_{(E,k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})}(t,x,y,z)\ \in\ V^{1}\ ,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}^{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=\mathtt{e}_{-k}(x)=\mathtt{e}_{(-E,-k_{x},-k_{y},-k_{z})}(t,x,y,z)\ \in\ V^{2}\ ,

(5.21)

defined via four-vectors
x
in position space and standard four-momenta
k
. These basis vectors are indeed dual with respect to the cyclic structure (
5.2
):

\displaystyle\langle\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}}\,,\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,(k_{1}-k_{2})\cdot x}=(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(k_{1}-k_{2})\ .

Let us now discuss the standard BV formalism for
\varPhi_{\star}^{3}
-theory in this basis, and compare the results with the calculations performed thus far in the cylindrical harmonic basis. There are two places where the change of basis is relevant:

•

The interacting BV master action
\mathcal{S}^{\tt plwa}_{\rm int}\in\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])
from (
3.5
) in the plane wave basis is defined by

\mathcal{S}^{\tt plwa}_{\rm int}:=-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle\zeta\,,\,\mu^{{\rm ext}}_{2}(\zeta,\zeta)\rangle^{\rm ext}\ ,

(5.22)

where now the contracted coordinate functions
\zeta\in\mathrm{Sym}(V[2])\otimes V
are constructed as combinations of plane waves

\displaystyle\zeta=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \big(\mathtt{e}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{e}^{k}+\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{e}_{k}\big)\ .

•

Homological perturbation theory now constructs the
n
-point quantum correlation functions (
3.4
) in momentum space as

\widetilde{G}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\,\mathsf{P}\,\big((-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}-\{\mathcal{S}^{\tt plwa}_{\rm int},-\}\,\mathsf{H})^{m}\,(\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot\mathtt{e}^{p_{n}})\big)\ ,

(5.23)

with insertions of the plane wave basis of antifields
\mathtt{e}^{p}
from (
5.21
) as external states.

To describe these modifications explicitly, following
[
12
]
we deform the addition law of momenta according to the deformed coproducts of the Cartesian components of the momentum operator in (
2.2
). We define the noncommutative associative star-sum
k+_{\star}p
of momenta
k,p\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
through

\mathtt{e}_{k+_{\star}p}:=\mathtt{e}_{k}\star\mathtt{e}_{p}\ .

This binary operation deforms the additive abelian group
(\mathbbm{R}^{4},+)
to the non-abelian group
(\mathbbm{R}^{4},+_{\star})
, with unit
0
and where the inverse of
k
with respect to
+_{\star}
is again
-k
.

Explicitly, the star-sums of two and three momenta read as
[
12
]

\displaystyle k+_{\star}p

\displaystyle={\mathsf{\Lambda}}(p_{z})\,k+{\mathsf{\Lambda}}(-k_{z})\,p\ ,

(5.24)

\displaystyle k+_{\star}p+_{\star}q

\displaystyle={\mathsf{\Lambda}}(p_{z}+q_{z})\,k+{\mathsf{\Lambda}}(-k_{z}+q_{z})\,p+{\mathsf{\Lambda}}(-k_{z}-p_{z})\,q\ ,

(5.25)

where the rotation matrix

{\mathsf{\Lambda}}(p_{z})={\small\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&\cos\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{z}\big)&\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{z}\big)&0\\
0&-\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{z}\big)&\cos\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{z}\big)&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{matrix}\right)}\normalsize

implements a rotation through angle
\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{z}
in the
(x,y)
-plane of momentum space. While the star-sum does not affect the components
(E,k_{z})
(they are summed in the usual abelian way), it modifies the addition law of the components
(k_{x},k_{y})
.

The deformed momentum conservation laws implied by (
2.2
) are then implemented by the Dirac distribution

\displaystyle\delta(k_{1}+_{\star}\cdots+_{\star}k_{n})=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{{\rm d}^{4}x}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x}\star\cdots\star{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{n}\cdot x}\ .

(5.26)

By the cyclicity property (
2.6
), it satisfies

\displaystyle\delta(k_{1}+_{\star}k_{2}+_{\star}\cdots+_{\star}k_{n})=\delta(k_{2}+_{\star}\cdots+_{\star}k_{n}+_{\star}k_{1})=\delta\big(k_{1}+(k_{2}+_{\star}\cdots+_{\star}k_{n})\big)\ .

In particular,
\delta(k_{1}+_{\star}k_{2})=\delta(k_{1}+k_{2})
.

Calculating as previously thereby leads to the interaction functional (
5.22
) in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\tt plwa}_{\rm int}=\int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{4})^{\times 3}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ V_{\tt plwa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ \mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\ ,

where the vertex (
5.6
) changes to a star-sum of momenta implemented by a Dirac distribution

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\tt plwa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})&=-\frac{g}{3!}\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(k_{1}+_{\star}k_{2}+_{\star}k_{3})\ .\end{split}

(5.27)

In particular, in this basis the noncommutativity of the interaction is no longer captured by a simple Moyal-like phase factor, but rather by a vertex which is formally identical to that of the commutative theory with a deformed momentum conservation law.

Two-point function at one-loop

Performing identical calculations to before but now in the plane wave basis, the free scalar field theory gives rise to the two-point function

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}})={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2})\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ .

(5.28)

The diagrams contributing to the one-loop corrections to (
5.28
) are the same as in the cylindrical harmonic basis, and again we just present the results of the calculations for the planar and non-planar contributions to the one-particle irreducible two-point function at one-loop.

Planar contribution.

Starting from (
5.11
), the typical planar diagram contribution to the one-loop two-point function is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{planar}}&=\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\times\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)\ .\end{split}

(5.29)

The first loop propagator contains an apparent noncommutative contribution in the form of a star-sum of external and internal momenta. A similar behaviour for the planar diagram contribution to the one-loop four-point function in the standard
\varPhi^{4}_{\star}
-theory was observed in
[
27
,
43
]
.

Let us look more closely at (
5.29
).
Using (
5.24
) and converting to polar coordinates in the
(p_{x},p_{y})
-plane, with
p_{ix}=\alpha_{i}\cos(\vartheta_{i})
and
p_{iy}=\alpha_{i}\sin(\vartheta_{i})
(see Appendix
A
), the Minkowski norm of the star-sum
(-p_{1})+_{\star}k
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=E_{(-p_{1})+_{\star}k}^{2}-\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)^{2}_{x}-\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)^{2}_{y}-\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)^{2}_{z}

\displaystyle=(E_{k}-E_{1})^{2}-(k_{z}-p_{1z})^{2}-\big(\alpha^{2}_{1}+\alpha^{2}_{k}\big)+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{k}\cos\big(\vartheta_{k}-\vartheta_{1}-\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,(p_{1z}-k_{z})\big)\ .

Written in cylindrical coordinates, the loop integral in (
5.29
) is then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}E_{k}\,\mathrm{d}k_{z}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,\frac{\alpha_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}}{2\pi}\ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{k}}{2\pi}\ \frac{1}{E^{2}_{k}-k^{2}_{z}-\alpha^{2}_{k}-m_{+}^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\frac{1}{(E_{k}-E_{1})^{2}-(k_{z}-p_{1z})^{2}-\big(\alpha^{2}_{1}+\alpha^{2}_{k}\big)+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{k}\cos\big(\vartheta_{k}-\vartheta_{1}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\,(p_{1z}-k_{z})\big)-m_{+}^{2}}\ .

The integrand is periodic with respect to
\vartheta_{k}
, and the angular integral is over
\vartheta_{k}\in\mathbbm{R}/2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
. Therefore, since
\mathbbm{R}/2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
is an
\mathbbm{R}
-module, the integral does not depend on arbitrary constant translations of
\vartheta_{k}
. If we choose to translate
\vartheta_{k}\rightarrow\vartheta_{k}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\,(p_{1z}-k_{z})
, we eliminate the dependence on the deformation parameter
\lambda
and restore the commutative result

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{planar}}&=\frac{1}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}\big(k-p_{1}\big)\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)\ .\end{split}

In this way we showed that the planar contribution to the one-loop two-point function is the same as the commutative result (up to a symmetry factor). This is consistent with the planar equivalence theorem
[
33
]
and the result (
5.13
).

Non-planar contribution.

Starting from (
5.12
) the typical non-planar diagram contribution to the one-loop two-point function is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}&=\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)\,\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(k)\\[-5.0pt]
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta\big(-p_{2}+_{\star}(-k)+_{\star}(-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)\ .\end{split}

(5.30)

The non-planar correction (
5.30
) receives two noncommutative contributions:

•

The first one comes from the Dirac distribution: it represents the deformed conservation law of momentum in the
(x,y)
-plane, i.e. of the radial momentum. This contribution has as its origin the star-product of plane waves, and is analogous to the phase factors appearing in the non-planar contribution to the corresponding correlator in the cylindrical harmonic basis (
5.14
).

•

The second one comes from the star-sum of momenta in a loop propagator and is specific to the
\lambda
-Minkowski (and
\rho
-Minkowski) space
[
27
,
43
]
.

Following
[
12
]
, one can check that the ultraviolet behaviour of (
5.30
) is indeed improved at any non-exceptional external momentum
\lambda\,p_{1z}\notin 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
. The explicit form of the Dirac distribution

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\delta\big(-p_{2}+_{\star}(-k)+_{\star}(-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \frac{1}{4\sin^{2}\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\delta\Big(k_{x}-\frac{p_{1y}+p_{2y}}{2\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,k_{z}\big)+\frac{p_{1x}-p_{2x}}{2\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,k_{z}\big)\Big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\delta\Big(k_{y}+\frac{p_{1x}+p_{2x}}{2\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\cos\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,k_{z}\big)+\frac{p_{1y}-p_{2y}}{2\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\sin\big(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,k_{z}\big)\Big)\end{split}

(5.31)

enables integration of the
(k_{x},k_{y})
components of the loop momentum
k
leaving an overall contribution of order
\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,\frac{{\rm d}E_{k}\,{\rm d}k_{z}}{k^{4}}
in the ultraviolet limit, which converges.

In order to compare the results (
5.30
) and (
5.14
) explicitly, we look more closely into the special case when
\alpha_{1}=0
. The deformation of the loop momentum can then be simplified due to the form of the deformed momentum conservation law (
5.31
) which gives

\displaystyle\big((-p_{1})+_{\star}k\big)^{2}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}=(E_{k}-E_{1})^{2}-(k_{z}-p_{1z})^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{2}_{2}}{4\sin^{2}\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\ .

The contribution of the non-planar diagram (
5.30
) then reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\normalsize&=\frac{1}{6}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})}{4\sin^{2}\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z}\big)}\\
&\quad\,\times\int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,{\rm d}E_{k}\,{\rm d}k_{z}\ \frac{1}{(E_{k}-E_{1})^{2}-(k_{z}-p_{1z})^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{2}_{2}}{4\sin^{2}(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z})}-m_{+}^{2}}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\frac{1}{E^{2}_{k}-k^{2}_{z}-\frac{\alpha^{2}_{2}}{4\sin^{2}(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{2z})}-m_{+}^{2}}\ .\end{split}

The loop integral is now formally identical to (
4.28
) and it can be evaluated in a similar manner. The end result is finite and after integration over the Feynman parameter
x\in[0,1]
it is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\,\Big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\pi}{12}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\Big(-\frac{g}{3!}\Big)^{2}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\frac{\displaystyle\tan^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\sin^{2}(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})}{\alpha_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,\big(4m^{2}-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})\big)}}}{\big|\sin\big(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\big|\,\sqrt{\big(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2}\big)\,\big(\alpha_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}(\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z})\,\big(4m^{2}-(E_{1}^{2}-p_{1z}^{2})\big)\big)}}\ .\end{split}

(5.32)

This is precisely the same result we found for the non-planar contribution in the cylindrical harmonic basis (
5.19
). In particular, the noncommutative contribution to the non-planar correction again removes the logarithmic ultraviolet divergence and gives a finite result for all
\lambda\,p_{1z}\notin 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
. Note that in both (
5.19
) and (
5.32
) the external momentum is not conserved in the
(x,y)
-plane, due to the absence of Dirac distributions
\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})
and
\delta(p_{1x}+p_{2x})\,\delta(p_{1y}+p_{2y})
(equivalently
\delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\,\delta(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}-\pi)
) in (
5.19
) and (
5.32
) correspondingly, reflecting the breaking of translational invariance by the noncommutative deformation.

On the other hand, in the limit
\alpha_{1}\to 0
and
\lambda\,p_{1z}\in 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
the contribution (
5.30
) reduces to the planar contribution (
5.29
). As in the planar diagram contribution, the logarithmic ultraviolet divergence reappears in the periodic infrared limit, and therefore we confirm that periodic UV/IR mixing is also present in the standard BV-quantized
\varPhi^{3}_{\star}
-theory represented in the basis of plane wave states.

5.6
Plane waves versus cylindrical harmonics

Our results for the two-point function at one-loop order in the two different bases, the planar (
5.13
) and non-planar (
5.14
) diagram contributions in the basis of cylindrical harmonic states, and the planar (
5.29
) and non-planar (
5.30
) diagram contributions in basis of plane wave states, agree precisely. The planar diagrams are logarithmically ultraviolet divergent, just as in the commutative case. The non-planar diagrams are ultraviolet finite, but in the limit
\lambda\,p_{1z}\in 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
they reduce to the corresponding planar diagrams and the ultraviolet divergence reappears, resulting in periodic UV/IR mixing. To better understand the relation between these results, we shall now compare the computations in these two different bases. We will decompose our building blocks — the vertex (
5.27
) and contractions, i.e. the free propagator (
5.28
) — given in terms of plane waves into the corresponding objects given in terms of cylindrical harmonics.

Recall that plane waves are characterized by quadruples of momenta
(E_{k},k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})
in Cartesian coordinates, or equivalently by quadruples of momenta
(E_{k},\alpha_{k},\vartheta_{k},k_{z})
in cylindrical coordinates, where

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=\sqrt{k^{2}_{x}+k^{2}_{y}}\qquad,\qquad\vartheta_{k}=\tan^{-1}\Big(\frac{k_{y}}{k_{x}}\Big)

(5.33)

are the radial momentum and the polar angle respectively in the
(k_{x},k_{y})
-plane. Cylindrical harmonics are instead characterised by quadruples of momenta
(E_{k},\alpha_{k},\ell_{k},k_{z})
, where
\ell_{k}\in\mathbbm{Z}
is the angular momentum in the
(k_{x},k_{y})
-plane.

Plane waves in Cartesian coordinates can be decomposed into cylindrical harmonics according to (
2.12
) as

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{k}=\sum_{\ell_{k}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,{\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{k}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{k}\,\vartheta_{k}}\ \mathtt{c}_{k}\ ,

(5.34)

where the corresponding basis states of the vector space of fields in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra are

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{e}_{(E_{k}\,,\,k_{x}\,,\,k_{y}\,,\,k_{z})}(t,x,y,z)={\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,E_{k}\,t+{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{x}\,x+{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{y}\,y+{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{z}\,z}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{c}_{(E_{k}\,,\,\alpha_{k}\,,\,\ell_{k}\,,\,k_{z})}(t,r,\varphi,z)=J_{\ell_{k}}(\alpha_{k}\,r)\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,\ell_{k}\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\,E_{k}\,t+\mathrm{i}\,k_{z}\,z}\ ,

with the coordinate transformation (
5.33
) understood.
The relation (
5.34
) is the transformation between basis states and it suggests that there is a general transformation between the corresponding objects in both bases. When multiple momenta are involved, we abbreviate subscripts
{}_{k_{i}}
by
i
in what follows.

Let us start with the free propagator of the theory in the plane wave basis (
5.28
), which is an elementary contraction

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}

\displaystyle=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}})={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2})\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \mathtt{e}_{p_{1}}\cdot\mathtt{e}_{p_{2}}\ .

Decomposing the plane waves under the integral according to (
5.34
) allows us to perform the integral over the spacetime coordinate
x
in its cylindrical form as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\ {\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}}(p_{1})\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}}\,{\rm d}t\,{\rm d}z\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,{\rm d}r\ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\,{\rm d}\varphi\ \sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})\,\varphi}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,(E_{1}+E_{2})\,t}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,(p_{1z}+p_{2z})\,z}\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ .

The integrals over the coordinates
(t,\varphi,z)
trivially yield delta-functions, while the integral over
r
is the integral of two Bessel functions leading to conservation of radial momentum. Comparing the result with the free propagator of the theory in the cylindrical basis (
5.7
)
proves the transformation relation among free propagators given in different bases:

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}}\ \widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}\ .

(5.35)

Note the implicit dependence on momenta on either side of this transformation:
\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}
refers to the free propagator in the plane wave basis with momenta
p_{i}=(E_{i},p_{ix},p_{iy},p_{iz})
in Cartesian coordinates, or equivalently
p_{i}=(E_{i},\alpha_{i},\vartheta_{i},p_{iz})
in cylindrical coordinates after applying (
5.33
), while
\widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}
refers to the free propagator in the basis of cylindrical harmonics with momenta of the form
p_{i}=(E_{i},\alpha_{i},\ell_{i},p_{iz})
.

Next we compare the interaction vertices in these two bases. The vertex in the basis of plane waves (
5.27
) contains the deformed momentum conservation law, expressed as the Dirac distribution in (
5.26
):

\displaystyle V_{\tt plwa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=-\frac{g}{3!}\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(k_{1}+_{\star}k_{2}+_{\star}k_{3})=-\frac{g}{3!}\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{4}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \mathtt{e}_{k_{1}}\star\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}}\star\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}}\ .

After decomposing plane waves into cylindrical harmonics by (
5.34
), the action of the twist is straightforward since it is diagonal with respect to the basis of cylindrical harmonics. It is easy to extract phase factors and convert the star-product to a pointwise product. The integral over the spacetime coordinate
x
can again be performed over
(t,\varphi,z)
resulting in delta-functions, while the integral over
r
is the integral of three Bessel functions. It is easy to identify the result with the vertex from (
5.6
) given in the basis of cylindrical harmonics, and we arrive at the transformation

\displaystyle V_{\tt plwa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{3}\,\vartheta_{3}}\ V_{\tt cyl}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ .

(5.36)

We now decompose the one-loop two-point planar diagram (
5.29
) into cylindrical harmonics. We start from a typical contribution to
\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\rm planar}
of the form

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{4})^{\times 3}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{4})^{\times 3}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}q_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ (-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,V_{\tt plwa}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ V_{\tt plwa}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times{\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{q_{1}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{q_{3}})\ {\Delta}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,(\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot\mathtt{e}^{q_{2}})\ .

Implementing the transformations of contractions (
5.35
) and vertices (
5.36
), after some extensive calculations this results in the diagram (
5.29
) decomposed in terms of the diagram (
5.13
) in the basis of cylindrical harmonics:

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\rm planar}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}}\ \widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\rm planar}\ .

We have checked explicitly that similar relations hold for the one-loop two-point non-planar diagram as well as for the tree-level three-point function:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}&=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}}\ \widetilde{C}_{2}(p_{1},p_{2})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}_{\text{non-planar}}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\widetilde{G}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}&=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{2}\,\vartheta_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{3}\,\vartheta_{3}}\ \widetilde{C}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}\ .\end{split}

(5.37)

From these results we conjecture that these basis transformations hold in general, that is, for any
n
-point function at any
l
-loop level. This in turn relates the all orders
n
-point correlation functions (
5.23
) and (
5.3
) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\in\mathbbm{Z}}\ {\mathrm{i}}^{\,\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{n}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{1}\,\vartheta_{1}-\cdots-{\mathrm{i}}\,\ell_{n}\,\vartheta_{n}}\ \widetilde{C}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\ .

As an application of these Fourier series transformations, we can gain some physical insight into how the momentum conservation laws are related between these bases. Let us go back to the tree-level three-point function, which in the bases of plane waves and cylindrical harmonics respectively reads as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{G}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\frac{g}{3!}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{3})\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+_{\star}p_{2}+_{\star}p_{3})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\widetilde{C}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\frac{g}{3!}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{3})\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\lambda}{2}\,(p_{1z}\,\ell_{2}-p_{2z}\,\ell_{1})}\ \delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},0}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times(2\pi)^{3}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z}+p_{3z})\ \mathtt{F}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\ .\end{split}

(5.38)

These three-point functions are related according to (
5.37
).

Consider the special case where
p_{1}=(E_{1},0,0,p_{1z})
, i.e. with purely axial momentum
\alpha_{1}=0
.
Inserting the explicit form of
\widetilde{C}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}
from (
5.38
) into (
5.37
) and using (
4.20
) results in

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}}}\big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar)^{2}\,\frac{g}{3!}\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{1})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{2})\ \widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(p_{3})\,(2\pi)^{4}\ \delta(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\ \delta(p_{1z}+p_{2z}+p_{3z})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\frac{\delta(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})}{\alpha_{2}}\ \delta\big(\vartheta_{2}-\vartheta_{3}-\pi+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}\big)\ .

From (
5.38
) we conclude that the deformed conservation of momentum encoded by the Dirac distribution
\delta(p_{1}+_{\star}p_{2}+_{\star}p_{3})\big|_{\alpha_{1}=0}
can be understood as a modification of the momentum conservation law in the
(p_{x},p_{y})
-plane by a shift of the relative polar angle between the momenta by
\frac{\lambda}{2}\,p_{1z}
.

6
Outlook and discussion

In this paper we applied the algebraic BV quantization method to scalar
\varPhi^{3}
-theory on
\lambda
-Minkowski space, introducing noncommutativity in two distinct ways. Although both approaches lead to identical Maurer-Cartan functionals, equivalent to the classical action of noncommutative
\varPhi^{3}
-theory, their quantization schemes differ thus leading to two distinct quantum field theories.

Both theories were expressed in a novel fashion through the spectral decomposition of scalar fields in a basis of cylindrical harmonics, but for different reasons. Braided BV quantization requires the retract maps to be equivariant under the symmetries of the underlying noncommutative spacetime, naturally selecting as basis vectors the
U_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathfrak{a}
-invariant eigenmodes of the Klein-Gordan operator in cylindrical coordinates. In other words, braided BV quantization diagonalises the angular twist by construction. On the other hand, standard BV quantization can be performed in any basis, without restriction, but we argued that the cylindrical harmonic basis is more natural and informative to use. For comparison, standard BV quantization was performed in both the cylindrical harmonic and plane wave bases, with precisely the same results, and the respective correlation functions were explicitly related to one another.

It would be interesting to extend our analysis based on cylindrical harmonics to more intricate quantum field theories on
\lambda
-Minkowski space, and in particular to theories with gauge symmetries. An obvious extension would be to noncommutative quantum electrodynamics. The explicit analysis of quantum correlators for theories with interactions of quartic or higher order, such as scalar
\varPhi^{4}
-theory and Yang-Mills theory, faces an immediate potential obstacle: to our knowledge, the radial momentum integrals of products of four or more cylindrical Bessel functions of the first kind are not explicitly known in generality. As a toy model to start with, one could investigate Chern-Simons theory on a spatial section
\mathbbm{R}^{3}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{4}
of
\lambda
-Minkowski space in order to decipher the role of cylindrical harmonics in a theory with non-abelian cubic gauge interactions.

The results of this paper, though model specific, have highlighted at least two general features of noncommutative field theories:

•

There are two very different quantization schemes. The braided theory is based on a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra with explicit noncommutativity. The Braided Wick Theorem compensates for the noncommutative contribution to the interaction vertex, leading to a clear absence of noncommutativity inside loop integrals, and hence of non-planar diagrams. This is in accordance with results from
[
7
]
for the case of the Moyal twist deformation. Standard BV quantization, on the other hand, is based on a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra with implicit noncommutativity and reproduces the same results obtained by the standard Feynman quantization of noncommutative scalar field theory.

•

Harmonic analysis based on the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}=\mathbbm{R}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(2)
of symmetries of
\lambda
-Minkowski space greatly simplifies calculations and presents results in a form more amenable to physical interpretation. For the Moyal twist, the harmonics are precisely the plane waves associated to the Lie algebra of translations
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
. Using the same basis for the angular twist leads to deformed momentum conservation laws which partially obscure some features of the quantum field theory, such as the planar equivalence theorem of
[
33
]
which holds for a broad class of twists based on subalgebras of the Poincaré algebra
\mathfrak{iso}(1,3)
. The basis of cylindrical harmonics, on the other hand, encodes noncommutativity through Moyal-like phase factors in interaction vertices and captures the consequences of the violation of standard momentum conservation in a precise fashion. By Frobenius’ Theorem, any abelian Drinfel’d twist can be cast into a Moyal-like form through a local change of coordinates, hence investigations of other twist-deformed field theories in this manner are likewise possible, at least in principle.

Appendix A
Cylindrical Bessel functions of the first kind

In this appendix we summarize some properties of the cylindrical Bessel functions
J_{\ell}(w)
for
\ell\in\mathbbm{Z}
and
w\in\mathbbm{C}
, along with some results on integrals of products of Bessel functions relevant for our calculations in the main text. Further details can be found in
[
5
,
28
,
21
,
23
]
and
[
1
, Chapter 10]
.

The Bessel function
J_{\ell}(w)
admits an integral representation along the real line as

\displaystyle J_{\ell}(w)=\frac{1}{\pi}\,\int_{0}^{\pi}\,{\rm d}\sigma\ \cos(\ell\,\sigma-w\sin\sigma)\ .

(A.1)

It has the reflection property

\displaystyle J_{-\ell}(w)=(-1)^{\ell}\,J_{\ell}(w)\ .

For
\alpha_{i}\in(0,\infty)
, the Bessel functions obey the orthogonality relation

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\ J_{\ell}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\,J_{-\ell}(\alpha_{2}\,r)=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\ .

(A.2)

The integration of the product of three Bessel functions is given by
[
28
, eq. (40)]

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ J_{-\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{3}\,r)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 113.81102pt=\int_{[0,2\pi)^{\times 3}}\,\frac{{\rm d}\vartheta_{1}\,{\rm d}\vartheta_{2}\,{\rm d}\vartheta_{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ \delta(\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2}+\vec{\alpha}_{3})\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,\ell_{1}\,(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{3})+\mathrm{i}\,\ell_{2}\,(\vartheta_{2}-\vartheta_{3})}\ ,\end{split}

(A.3)

which can be explicitly evaluated to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ J_{-\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{3}\,r)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad=\begin{cases}\ 0\ ,&\quad\alpha_{3}>\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\quad\text{or}\quad 0<\alpha_{3}<|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}|\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ \displaystyle(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\ \frac{\cos(\ell_{1}\,\upsilon_{2}-\ell_{2}\,\upsilon_{1})}{\pi\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}\sin(\upsilon_{3})}\ ,&\quad|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}|<\alpha_{3}<\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\ ,\>\upsilon_{1}+\upsilon_{2}+\upsilon_{3}=\pi\ .\end{cases}\end{split}

(A.4)

The notation used in the formulas (
A.3
) and (
A.4
), and in particular the definition of the angles
\vartheta_{i}
and
\upsilon_{i}
, may be represented pictorially as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\small\hbox to194.91pt{\vbox to153.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.6941pt\lower-18.6941pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(A.5)

Here we have chosen auxiliary
x
and
y
axes for illustration purposes only. None of our results depends on that choice since the angular twist respects cylindrical symmetry.

In (
A.5
) we have adopted the following conversion. By promoting
\alpha>0
to the vector
\vec{\alpha}=\alpha\,\vec{e}_{r}
, where
\vec{e}_{r}
is the unit radial vector in the
(x,y)
-plane, we can satisfy the condition under which the integral in (
A.4
) is non-trivial by the choice

\vec{\alpha}_{1}+\vec{\alpha}_{2}+\vec{\alpha}_{3}=\vec{0}\ .

(A.6)

The constraint (
A.6
) represents the triangle in (
A.5
), in which the trigonometric laws of sines and cosines apply. In particular, from the area of the triangle we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}\sin(\upsilon_{3})=\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{3}\sin(\upsilon_{2})=\alpha_{2}\,\alpha_{3}\sin(\upsilon_{1})\ ,

along with

\displaystyle 2\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}\cos(\upsilon_{3})=\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}-\alpha_{3}^{2}\ ,

as well as two analogous cosine laws corresponding to cyclic permutations of the indices
(123)
.

The integral (
A.4
) is non-zero only when all
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
. When one sets
\alpha_{3}=0
, one can use (
A.1
) to deduce

J_{\ell}(0)=\delta_{\ell,0}\ ,

(A.7)

which implies
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=0
. Then the integral (
A.2
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\ J_{\ell_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\,r)\ J_{\ell_{2}}(\alpha_{2}\,r)\ J_{-\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}}(0)=\delta_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},0}\ \frac{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}{\alpha_{1}}\ \delta(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\ .

(A.8)
